<meta>This page serves as the frontispiece or decorative title page of the manuscript. A small pencil notation "R" is visible in the top-left corner of the central illustration.</meta>

# HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "PHILOSOPHIA · HERMETICA"; the term refers to a tradition of spiritual wisdom, alchemy, and mysticism attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note>

<note>The central emblem on this page is a dense collection of alchemical and <term>Rosicrucian</term> (a secret mystical brotherhood) symbols. 

At the heart of the image is a <term>Pelican in her Piety</term>. In medieval and alchemical lore, the pelican was believed to wound its own breast to feed its young with its blood, symbolizing sacrifice and the "Great Work" of purifying the soul. The bird stands upon a silver <term>crescent moon</term> (representing the feminine principle or the metal silver) which in turn rests upon a white <term>cube</term>. The cube represents the stability of the physical world or the "salt" of the elements, and it is decorated with four <term>red roses</term> arranged in a cross, signifying the blooming of spiritual life. 

Encircling this scene is the <term>Ouroboros</term>, a serpent devouring its own tail, an ancient symbol for the infinite cycle of nature and the unity of all things—often summarized by the phrase "All is One." Above the entire arrangement shines a <term>golden sun</term>, representing the masculine principle, enlightenment, and the successful completion of the alchemical process.</note>

<summary>This frontispiece introduces the work through a complex alchemical emblem, synthesizing symbols of sacrifice, stability, and the eternal cycle of nature. It establishes the text's foundation in the Hermetic and Rosicrucian traditions.</summary>

<keywords>Hermeticism, alchemy, Ouroboros, Pelican in her piety, Rosicrucianism, symbolism, frontispiece, Great Work</keywords>

# PREFACE

[Wisdom] and your prudence it was owed, that as you *were once the Master of the Court with the highest praise; and are now known as the most upright President of the <term>High Court</term> <note>original: "Dicasterij", the central judicial and administrative council of the Duchy</note>,* you should bear the name of a **Christian Cato** <note>Marcus Porcius Cato, or Cato the Elder, was a Roman statesman famous for his strict morality; Andreae is calling his patron a modern, Christian model of such integrity</note> from me, a man of whatever station I may be. Therefore I pray, as reverently as I can, that you would not deny me your judgment; if I am supported by it, I shall move all good men to agreement and consensus. I pray to God that, since no earthly thing can equal your lifelong cares for the fatherland, you may receive a most abundant reward and prize in Heaven. Farewell in Christ, the glory of our nobility.

->To Your Excellence,<-

->the devoted servant with<-
->all obedience and respect,<-

->Johann Valentin Andreae.<-

<summary>Johann Valentin Andreae concludes his dedication to Wilhelm von Remchingen, hailing him as a "Christian Cato" for his judicial integrity and praying for his eternal reward after a life of service to the state.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Valentin Andreae, Wilhelm von Remchingen, Cato the Elder, High Court, piety, Christian nobility, Württemberg</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the main body of the work following the dedicatory preface. The text introduces the narrator's transition from a "Pilgrim" lost in the world to a "Citizen" restored to his true self through God.</meta>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut headpiece featuring symmetrical floral and foliate scrolls.</image-desc>

# JESUS.

## I.

->*The Citizen*<-

Driven about and exhausted by the infinite windings and changes of error, a wretched **Pilgrim** <note>original: "Peregrinus". The "Pilgrim" is a central figure in Andreae's philosophy, representing the soul searching for truth in a confusing world</note>—at a time when Satan, the Prince of the **Worldly Labyrinth** <note>The "Labyrinth" was a popular 17th-century metaphor for the vanity and confusion of secular life, which Andreae explored extensively in his other writings</note>, was already threatening the very worst—by the favor of God, I have been restored to myself, from whom I had wandered. For while I was seeking myself elsewhere than within, and investigating by many wanderings outside of me that which was actually inside, I was called back into the **innermost shrine** <note>original: "penetralia", referring to the most private or sacred inner parts of a temple or, metaphorically, the depths of the human heart</note> of my heart by God (to whom alone this power belongs). Now, I have clearly perceived, and with infinite joy, that I am at home; that I am a citizen in my true Fatherland, and no longer a pilgrim. For here, the origin of my kind from the divine **breath** <note>original: "afflatu"</note> was clear, as was the structure of my body from the hand of God, a new adoption from God's mercy, and a new discipline from...

<summary>The author describes his spiritual turning point, moving from the state of a lost "Pilgrim" in the world's labyrinth to a "Citizen" who finds God and his true self within his own heart.</summary>
<keywords>Pilgrim, Worldly Labyrinth, Satan, heart, spiritual restoration, divine breath, citizenship, Johann Valentin Andreae</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition of the narrator, who has moved from being a "Pilgrim" lost in the world to a "Citizen" of God’s kingdom. Having established that his true identity is divine, he now explores the internal "sanctuary" of the human soul as a reflection of the entire universe.</meta>

The presence of God—namely, that I belong to God and not to this World—became known to me. Thus, there was no need to seek, question, or listen to God and other divine matters anywhere other than in the **sanctuary of the heart** <note>original: "in pectoris sacrario". The author emphasizes internal spirituality and a personal relationship with the divine over external searching or worldly wisdom</note>. For He has arranged and enriched our **small world** <note>original: "minutum nostrum mundum". This refers to the "Microcosm," the philosophical idea that the human being is a miniature version of the entire universe</note> with such wisdom that it represents that **Greatest World** <note>The "Macrocosm," or the vast physical universe</note> perfectly, and possesses no fewer wonders.

For here, the craftsmanship of God, contracted into a kind of summary, expresses the movements, forces, and changes of Heaven and earth, but especially the conflicts and the intervention of **CHRIST** the reconciler. It fills the person who **KNOWS HIMSELF** <note>original: "SE NOVIT," referencing the ancient Greek maxim *gnothi seauton* (know thyself), which in this Christian context means recognizing one's divine origin and internal spiritual state</note> with the contemplation of infinite and most beautiful things, so that he may behold the convergence and the source of all things within his own center. 

It is truly a tragedy that such a great multitude of the human race walks about in ignorance of themselves, carrying around a **mechanism** <note>original: "machinam". In the 17th century, the human body and soul were often described as a "divine machine" or a complex, engineered wonder of God</note> so precious—yet it is not so much hidden as it is overwhelmed, obstructed, defiled, and rusted. I do not know if they simply lack someone to warn them; certainly, the deafness and blindness of many is so great that by no shouts...

<summary>The author describes the human being as a "Microcosm" containing all the wonders of the universe and the presence of God within the heart. He mourns that most people remain ignorant of their own internal spiritual "machinery," allowing it to fall into disrepair.</summary>

<keywords>Microcosm, Macrocosm, Know Thyself, sanctuary of the heart, Christ the reconciler, divine architecture, spiritual ignorance</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrator's transition from the external world to the internal spiritual life. Having established that God is found within the "sanctuary of the heart," the narrator now describes the visceral experience of being called by a divine voice to turn away from the world and enter his own soul.</meta>

# CHRISTIANUS

...could be overcome by no proofs. Whatever there is of it, by truly and simply reciting what I have seen and heard, I shall fulfill my duty in good and Christian faith regarding that which I have received within myself.

While I was still trembling and totally stunned by the ministers of Satan <note>The narrator describes a state of spiritual shock or trauma from his previous life in the "world" before his conversion</note>, a voice from behind cried out, more piercing and more vital: **ARISE** <note>original: "SURGE"</note>. This voice at once raised me to my feet, as it were, and animated me with new strength. And when I looked around, it cried out again: **COME** <note>original: "VENI"</note>. Soon, when I hesitated, it said: **BACKWARD** <note>original: "RETRO"</note>, and it completely turned me around and away—or more truly, it turned me inward <note>original: "introvertit". This is a key term in mystical theology, referring to the act of turning one's attention away from the physical senses and toward the inner life of the spirit</note>. 

Here, under a faint light, a very narrow **sanctuary** <term>sacrarium: a sacred place or inner shrine. In this context, it represents the "hidden room" of the soul where God dwells</term> appeared. It was humble in form, with a tiny door, admitting only a very small and shadowy light through the lowest openings. When I had entered it, at first my eyes grew dim; afterward, as my sight gradually returned, various half-eroded images of the **virtues** <note>original: "virtutum simulacra". These represent the moral qualities of the human soul which, in the author's view, have been damaged or "corroded" by human sin</note> appeared, of crude painting and sculpture. I saw, for instance, Wisdom, Justice, Temperance, Fortitude, Prudence, and the like. These were for the most part mutilated in their limbs and broken by some barbarian devastation. I saw...

<meta>This page continues the narrator's internal journey. Having turned away from the external world and toward the "sanctuary of the heart," the narrator begins to describe the profound realization that the human soul is a microcosm containing the wonders of the entire universe.</meta>

# THE CITIZEN

The presence of God—specifically, the fact that I belong to God and not to this World—became known to me. Therefore, there was no need to seek, question, or listen to God and other divine matters anywhere else but in the **sanctuary of the heart** <term>pectoris sacrario: the inner-most part of the human soul, viewed as a temple or hidden shrine where God dwells</term>. It is He who has arranged and enriched our "minute world" <note>The "minute world" refers to the concept of the *Microcosm*, the idea that a human being is a miniature version of the entire universe (the *Macrocosm*)</note> with such wisdom that it represents that Greatest World perfectly <note>original: "ad unguem," literally "to the fingernail," a Latin idiom for doing something with absolute precision</note>, and possesses no fewer wonders.

For here, the workmanship of God, compressed into a kind of brief summary, expresses the motions, powers, and cycles of Heaven and earth—but most especially the conflicts and the intervention of **CHRIST** the Mediator. It fills the man who **KNOWS HIMSELF** <note>A reference to the famous Greek maxim "Know Thyself," which the author applies here to the Christian duty of looking inward to find God</note> with the contemplation of infinite, beautiful things, so that he may behold within his own center the meeting-point of all things, and even their every departure.

It is therefore a matter of great sorrow that such a vast multitude of the human race walks about ignorant of themselves. They carry around a **mechanism** <term>machinam: in the 17th century, this word referred to any complex, divinely-designed structure, such as a clock or the human body and soul</term> so precious—yet it is not so much hidden as it is buried, obstructed, defiled, and rusted. I know not if they lack someone to warn them, but certainly the deafness and blindness of many is such that by no shouts...

<summary>The narrator reflects on the human soul as a microcosm of the universe, lamenting that most people live in ignorance of the divine "mechanism" within them. He emphasizes that Christ acts as the mediator within this internal sanctuary.</summary>

<keywords>microcosm, self-knowledge, sanctuary of the heart, Christ the Mediator, spiritual blindness, divine workmanship</keywords>

CHRISTIAN

<note>This page continues a sentence from the previous page regarding the certainty of his internal experience.</note> ...cannot be overcome by any proofs. Whatever the nature of it may be, I shall fulfill the task I have taken upon myself in good and Christian faith, by truly and simply recounting what I have seen and heard.

While I was still trembling and utterly stunned by the threats of **Satan** <note>original: "Satanæ"</note>, a voice from behind called out, more piercing and more vital: **ARISE** <note>original: "SURGE"</note>. This voice at once set me on my feet, as it were, and animated me with new strength. As I looked around, it called out again: **COME** <note>original: "VENI"</note>. Soon, as I hesitated, it said: **BACKWARD** <note>original: "RETRO"</note>, and it completely turned me around and away—or rather, to speak more truly, it turned me inward.

Here, under a dim and flickering light, a very narrow **sanctuary** <term>sacrarium: a holy place or shrine; here used to describe the inner-most part of the human spirit</term> appeared. It was humble in its form, with a tight little door, admitting only a small and shadowy light through the cracks. When I had entered there, at first my eyes grew dim; then, as my sight gradually returned, various half-corroded images of the **virtues** <note>In this allegory, the virtues are depicted as physical statues within the soul, reflecting the classical idea that the soul was originally built as a temple of moral excellence</note> appeared, of crude painting and sculpture. These were images of **wisdom**, **justice**, **temperance**, **fortitude**, **prudence**, and the like. Most of them were mutilated in their limbs and broken, as if by some barbaric devastation.

# CITIZEN

<meta>The text continues from the previous page's final incomplete word: "mutilated."</meta>

...saw also the dismantled wheels and circles of a certain **spherical machine** <note>original: "machinæ... sphæricæ"; this likely refers to an armillary sphere or an orrery, complex mechanical models used to represent the movements of the heavens</note>, a heap of various **armillary rings** <term>armillarum: metal rings used in astronomical instruments to model the celestial sphere</term>, and a scattering of axles and small gear-teeth—the **relics** and fragments of what was once an ingenious work, which no craftsman could ever restore. 

But I also looked upon plucked wings, smashed ladders, and split **pulleys** <note>original: "trochleas"</note>, the traces of some remarkable but futile effort; without an interpreter, all these things brought about a great deal of confusion. Thus, as if in a **second-hand market** <term>scrutario foro: a marketplace for old, broken, or junk items</term>, I wondered at the appearances and forms of so many useless and ruined things, entirely ignorant of the purpose for which this absurd heap had been assembled. 

Yet this place did not reek like the chambers of the **World** <note>original: "Mundi"; a reference to the secular or physical world the narrator has turned away from</note> recently did; instead, it gave off a sweet and pleasant scent. It was not terrifying, but rather it encouraged me and bid me to have good hope. Thus, free from my former anxiety, I contemplated the various paintings,

# CHRISTIAN

## II.
### *Christ.*

O unspeakable light, which suddenly appeared! For someone was descending through the vault of the **sanctuary** <term>sacrarium: a private chapel or holy place, here representing the inner soul</term> in a human form, indeed similar to us in flesh and limbs in every way, yet in his brilliance most clearly God. Since he was shining entirely, he illuminated the sanctuary with a far different light, and he opened and revealed the things that had been hidden from me until now. 

Here the images of the **virtues** <note>These are the same statues described as "mutilated" on the previous page; their restoration signifies the healing power of the divine presence.</note> reflected light like the purest crystal and gleamed with wonderful craftsmanship; indeed, they even began to move and come to life. Nor was any part left mutilated; everything was seen in its most complete and perfect state. 

Likewise, the spherical circles <note>original: "sphærici circuli"</note> returned into a most majestic **clockwork mechanism** <term>horologium: while often a clock, here it refers to the complex astronomical machine or orrery mentioned previously</term>, whose motions expressed this universe with astounding imitation and reflected the idea of **Divine Governance** <note>original: "regiminis Divini"; the idea that the universe is a machine perfectly ordered and maintained by God</note>. Nor did anything remain whose perfection and most evident use was not on display, so that it had to be admitted that everything was present and nothing was lacking. But the **God-man** <term>Theanthropus: from the Greek "theos" (God) and "anthropos" (man), referring to the dual nature of Christ</term> himself, in a white and shining robe...

<sig>A 5</sig>

<summary>The narrator witnesses a divine epiphany where Christ descends into the "sanctuary of the heart," miraculously restoring the broken statues of virtues and the complex mechanical models of the universe to their original perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Christ, epiphany, restoration, virtues, Divine Governance, Theanthropus, inner sanctuary, mechanical universe</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the vision of the God-man (Christ) descending into the heart's sanctuary. The previous page ended describing him in a white and shining robe.</meta>

# THE CITIZEN

...clothed, he brought with him a book and a cross by which he might establish the rule of knowing and acting <note>original: "sciendi, agendique regulam"; a framework for both belief and moral conduct</note>, which would be so much more pleasing to a person, as these did not match the volumes of the World’s laws in their massive size, nor did the beam of this cross match the weight of the World’s <term>Mundus: the secular realm of human society and sin, often personified as a deceptive or heavy-handed ruler</term> burdens. Instead, both were manageable even by a child's hand. 

His face, though most brilliant, nevertheless permitted human eyes to look upon it; it did not terrify but rather refreshed, and breathed forth altogether **PURE LOVE**, displaying a gentleness never before seen in the World. Thus, blushing with shame, I finally realized that this was the very opposite of the World, the one opposed to the World, whom my guide <note>The narrator realizes they were deceived by worldly perspectives earlier in the journey</note> had wickedly tried to make hateful to me by piling on so many insults, to make ridiculous by accusing him of so many follies, and to persuade me to flee and avoid by charging him with such harshness. He, therefore, who was gentleness itself, meekness itself, and clemency itself, spoke to me in this way:

> You have come at last, my Son and Brother, so that you might finally do those things which you heard from me long ago. For while countless people acknowledge and profess my name, very few

<summary>The narrator encounters Christ, who appears not as a terrifying judge but as a figure of pure love and gentleness, carrying a book and a cross that are light and accessible compared to the heavy burdens of the world.</summary>

->THE CHRISTIAN. 11<-

express [my name] quite well. There are many Christians, but few **Christ-bearers** <note>original: "Christoferi"; a play on the name Christopher, meaning those who "carry" Christ in their hearts</note>; many **Gospel-bearers** <note>original: "Evangeliophori"; those who carry the word but perhaps not the spirit</note>, but few **cross-bearers** <note>original: "crucigeri"; those who truly take up the burden of faith</note>. This is because the World <term>Mundus: the secular realm, often depicted in this era as a source of deception and spiritual weight</term> has so thoroughly persuaded people that it is enough merely to know what I have willed. Therefore, I embrace you, and kiss you, and bind you to myself, so that from this point forward you may both see me as I appear and hear me as I speak: you shall receive me as I teach and imitate me as I act; you shall attend to me as I show the way and follow me as I lead; you shall observe me as I persuade and obey me as I command; you shall look upon me as I work and emulate me as I suffer; and you shall love me when I am gentle and endure with me when I am harsh. 

In truth, you should possess neither my book without the cross, nor my cross without the book—that is, you should not hold my doctrine without my example and my life—and you shall be bound to me by the unbreakable bond of *Charity* <note>original: "Charitas"; referring to the highest form of divine love</note>, the fruit of which will be immense for you here: so that after disgrace you may possess honors, after pain, joy, after labor, rest, and after poverty, eternal treasures forever. At the same time, *Christ* stretched out his right hand, and having grasped mine, he bathed my whole being in divine dew; and once I was washed and refreshed by heavenly manna <note>The miraculous "bread from heaven" provided to the Israelites, here symbolizing spiritual nourishment</note>, he declared that I was *His own*,

commands me to be named and to remain; to whom I responded, not trembling as I once did in the World <term>Mundus: the secular or material realm</term>, but joyful with the confidence of a son: Take me for yourself, Jesus my king, and snatch me from the World. Say what you will, and grant that I may be able; command what you will, and grant that I may obey; impose what you will, and grant that I may bear it; train me as you will, and grant that I may be sufficient; give what you command, and command what you will <note>original: "da quod jubes, & jube quod vis"; a famous prayer of surrender by St. Augustine of Hippo</note>; I shall be nothing, so that you may be everything. While I am saying these things, the little chapel <note>original: "sacellum"; a small private shrine or sanctuary</note> which until now had been narrow, expands into a most majestic temple divided into many courtyards and displays a most vast image of this universe.

## III.
### *Philanthropy* <note>original: "Philantropia"; here used in its literal sense of "love for humanity"</note>

It was easy to understand that everything in this temple comes from the One and returns to the One. For just as they say a great multitude once hung from the mouth of Hercules <note>A reference to the "Gallic Hercules," an image from classical mythology where the god of strength leads people not by force, but by the "golden chains" of his eloquence stretching from his tongue to their ears</note>, so here the entire human race was seen connected to the Divine heart by tiny chains <note>original: "cathenis"</note>. For this is that center of Mercy, which throughout all ages [prepared] the Lamb...

<meta>catchword: Passover-</meta>

<summary>The narrator surrenders his will to Christ, causing his internal spiritual "chapel" to expand into a vast temple representing the universe. He then observes a vision of all humanity connected to the Divine heart through chains of love and mercy.</summary>

<keywords>surrender, divine will, St. Augustine, temple of the heart, Hercules, divine mercy, interconnectedness of humanity</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrator's vision within the majestic temple. On the previous page, the narrator observed the human race connected to the Divine heart by chains, centered on the Mercy of the "Lamb."</meta>

->CHRISTIAN<-

He destined the **Passover** <note>original: "Paschalem"; referring to the sacrifice of the Paschal Lamb, symbolizing Christ</note> to atone for the sins of Adam; this is that most merciful plan which receives all the faithful into grace; this is the summit of charity, which voluntarily descends to us and adapts itself to our needs; this is the most wise judgment, which distinguishes between the worthy and the unworthy. Therefore, the heart of God appeared to me as a **Magnet** of infinite power, which drew toward itself all those consecrated to Christ—who were placed upon the **needles of faith** <note>The author uses the metaphor of a compass needle. Just as a needle is magnetized by a "sacred stone" (lodestone) to find its direction, the believer's faith is "magnetized" by Christ to point toward God</note>—by that part where they had rubbed themselves against the sacred stone. Furthermore, it repelled that which was characterized by contact and contagion with the **World** <term>Mundus: the material or secular realm, often seen in this period as a source of spiritual corruption</term>.

From this, there was a single direction for everyone and a gaze toward that same region; they did not each follow the madness of their own heads, but were all ruled by a secret motor and were turned toward a single goal. Thus, they were not moved naturally—otherwise they would have slipped downward, which is a daily occurrence in the **World**—but supernaturally, so that they now willed something other than what they [naturally] will, and were capable of something other than what they [naturally] can do, specifically through the benefit of Divine love. For he who is drawn by the affection of sacred love scarcely contains himself, so far is it from...

### THE CHRISTIAN

This service is also performed far more safely for God than for the World; for He has no "secrets of state" <note>original: "status arcana"; referring to the hidden political agendas or intrigues of worldly rulers</note> in which we—ignorant and occupied with other matters—might serve. Rather, He embraces all things in the single secret of **Charity** <note>original: "Charitas"; the theological term for divine, selfless love</note>, so that, having eventually snatched us away from ourselves, He may preserve and deliver us to Himself, and gather us into heaven once we have been led out of the World.

It is a wonder that so few Christians value this immense benefit, and instead look toward other goals and other ends than Heaven while the use of this time—or rather the measured span of this captivity <note>The author portrays earthly life as a period of exile or "captivity" before returning to a heavenly home</note>—is still available to us. Instead, we should leave the world to the worldly, the earth to earthly things, vanity to the vain, and death to those who are mortal. We ought to work and struggle with every effort and the sweat of our brow toward our **calling**, which invites, draws, and spurs us toward the heavens, from our earliest years until our final delirium.

## IV.
### *The Divine Indwelling.*
<note>original: "Divina inhabitatio"; a theological concept referring to God living within the soul of a believer</note>

I have found nothing more fortunate here than misfortune, nothing wiser than ignorance, and nothing wealthier than poverty. For those whom **Fortune** favors, and to whom **Mercury** <note>The Roman god of commerce, eloquence, and trickery</note> and **Pluto** <note>The Roman god of wealth and the underworld</note> are well-disposed, are in a most wretched state; this is because they are filled with mere opinions and possess nothing of the truth at all. Therefore, only he is truly suited for God who, being empty of every earthly thing, makes room for heaven. Indeed, we must "not-know" so that we may know God; we must be "foolish" so that we may be wise in God; and we must be "in-need" so that we may possess God.

It is a marvel that we seek and guard with such great effort those very things which keep us away from God and hinder our progress. Because the World does not admit this—but rather babbles that these worldly things are actually beneficial for reaching heaven—it is all the more wicked. 

Here, the matter was shown to me differently. For when I had caught sight of an innumerable crowd of people journeying toward Heaven, I saw a most rigorous examination established at the Gate, which every single person had to undergo. Not only were their clothes shaken out, lest any **Vanity** <note>original: "Vanitas"; referring to pride or worthless worldly attachments</note> lay hidden and wrapped within them, but even their small bundles—

<summary>The author presents a series of spiritual paradoxes, arguing that worldly wealth and wisdom are barriers to God, and describes a vision of a rigorous "customs check" at the gates of Heaven where all vanity must be stripped away.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Indwelling, spiritual poverty, holy ignorance, Fortune, Mercury, Pluto, Vanity, Heaven's Gate, judgment</keywords>

were searched, so that nothing forbidden might remain; but what was truly unusual was that the very intestines, the head itself, and the heart were being emptied out, lest any impurity <note>original: "purulenti"; literally "pus-like" or "foul matter," used here to describe spiritual corruption</note> defile the seat of God. And although this was not done without pain, it was nevertheless managed so skillfully with heavenly medicines that it seemed to have increased life rather than diminished it.

And then, into the place of the foul-smelling liquid, a sacred flame <note>original: "sacra flamma"; representing the Holy Spirit or divine love</note> entered, which in a moment transformed the man into another. He himself then began to wonder how he could have ever endured such a stench, let alone valued it; and he laughed at his own insanity for having burdened his body with empty baggage. Indeed, it became a most delightful spectacle: in a single moment, something was achieved that no age could obtain here on earth, and a crude subject was changed into the most noble instrument. 

Stutterers surpassed eloquence; shepherds <note>original: "opiliones"</note> presided over the wise; fishermen <note>A traditional reference to the Apostles of Jesus, who were humble laborers transformed into leaders</note> convinced philosophers; and unlearned men <note>original: "idiotas"; used in the classical sense of "private persons" or those without formal education, rather than the modern insult</note> educated sophists. That is to say: foolishness was wise, weakness fought, and poverty abounded.

### THE CITIZEN IV. *The Divine Indwelling.*

I found here that nothing is happier than unhappiness, nothing wiser than ignorance, and nothing more opulent than poverty. For those whom Fortune <note>The Roman goddess of luck and fate</note> favors, and for whom Mercury <note>The god of commerce, trickery, and communication</note> and Pluto <note>The god of wealth and the underworld</note> are propitious, are in a most miserable state; this is because they are filled with mere opinion and possess nothing of the truth at all. Therefore, only he is suited to God who, being empty of all earthly things, has room for heaven. 

Indeed, one must "not know" so that we may know God; one must be a fool so that we may savor God <note>original: "sapiamus"; a play on the Latin word meaning both to be wise and to taste or perceive</note>; and one must be in want so that we may possess God. It is a wonder that we seek and guard with such great effort those things which keep us away from and hinder us from God. Because the World <note>original: "Mundus"; referring here to secular society and its values</note> does not admit this—but rather babbles that such things are beneficial for heaven—it is all the more wicked. 

Here, the truth was shown to me otherwise. For when I had caught sight of an innumerable crowd journeying toward Heaven, I saw a most rigorous examination established at the Gate, which had to be undergone by every single person. For not only were their clothes shaken out, lest any Vanity <note>original: "Vanitatis"; referring to empty pride or worldly futility</note> lay hidden wrapped within, but even their small bundles

Thus, whichever way you turn a true **Christian**, he is always consistent with himself. He does not value poverty less than wealth, nor honors more than disgrace; instead, he judges them as equally good and right, wherever he recognizes the hand and will of God.

For those things which the **World** calls by the names "good fortune" and "misfortune," he is utterly ignorant of, believing that all things which occur by Divine command are auspicious <note>original: "auspicata"; meaning favorable or divinely signaled</note>. Therefore, whether you wish a **Christian** to command or to obey, to teach or to learn, to possess abundance or to be in want, he will always walk with the same expression and remain with the same mental poise <term>æquilibrium: a state of inner balance or composure that is not disturbed by outside events</term>. For he knows that what he truly desires is not of this earth.

Meanwhile, however—just as among captives certain arrangements are established to pass the time <note>original: "tædij fallendi"; literally "for the sake of deceiving the boredom" or "beguiling the weariness" of exile</note>—it matters little to him what lot or role he receives in these temporary matters, since all things are insignificant when compared to his ultimate hopes. # CHRISTIANUS.

to honor, hear, follow, and love <note>completing the word *honorare* from the previous page</note> God, they have no desire to be among those who are injurious to God. This is an opinion that has deceived many: they promise themselves Divine protection even while surrounded by defilements, not realizing that we are only then exposed to Satan when we have driven away the **fellowship of the Angels** <note>original: "Angelorum contubernium"; refers to the intimate shared living space or companionship of these spirits</note> by the foulness of our shames.

Another noble benefit of this sacred society must not be kept silent. Indeed, this school surpasses all the schools of this earth. Earthly learning can only dig its way from the latter to the former <note>a reference to *a posteriori* reasoning, or working backward from effects to their causes</note>, and it can no more successfully judge things by conjectures than a blind man can judge the light of the Sun. But those spirits communicate the things they behold face-to-face and read within God himself to men—if it be permitted by the **Supreme Deity** <note>original: "supremum Numen"</note>—and even the future things of God...

# CITIZEN

...they reveal by His command. One can now see and name future kings and the leaders of the world, the struggles of the Church, the **venom of the Antichrist** <note>original: "Antichristi virus"</note>, and the alternating cycles of light and darkness—mysterious truths that no skill in astrology or shrewdness in divination could have ever uncovered. 

But even the deeper secrets of **Nature** <term>Nature: used here to refer to the physical world and the hidden laws governing it</term> are revealed through these teachers—secrets that would never have yielded to human effort alone. Ingenious inventions are provided, through which man often transcends his own limits and reaches toward the heavens. 

Indeed, all these things are such that the human race can never offer enough thanks to God. For we have been lifted out of this **mire** <note>original: "caeno"; literally filth or mud, used metaphorically for the base or corrupt state of material existence</note> by so many aids, sheltered by so many protections, and endowed with so many privileges and honors. Whoever neglects or scorns these things strips himself of **God's gifts** <note>original: "Dei muneribus"</note> and blessings, plunging himself back down into the filthy company of beasts.

# THE CHRISTIAN. VII.

But now the time had come for me to examine the particulars of the **sacred totality** <term>totality: original "universitas," referring here to the complete body of divine knowledge or the universal order</term>. There, first of all, I encountered the **citadel of truth**, constructed from the most solid and brilliant diamond, in a rectangular shape that was perfectly symmetrical on all sides. In the middle stood an invincible tower with the inscription: **"He Himself has spoken"** <note>original: "Ipse dixit"; a phrase used to denote absolute authority, emphasizing that the truth rests on God’s own word</note>. The gates of the citadel were three, bearing the titles: **Present, Past, and Future**. Who could recount all the other wonders to be found there?

They said that this citadel had been attacked more than a thousand times but never harmed; instead, it had shattered every army, no matter how numerous. Yet it did not make a show of its strength, and except for being **simple and straight** <note>original: "simplex, rectumque"</note>, it lacked all artificiality and outward elegance. I was stunned when I saw with what inviolable faith everything must be conducted here, how everything consists of the simplest words, and with what great brevity everything is handled. Because of this, the "truth" that boasts of itself in the world began to seem more filthy to me, with its trifles, its evasions, and its slippery... <note>the text cuts off mid-word: "lubri-" likely for "lubricitas" or slipperiness/deceit</note>

## IX.

## X.

### *The Decalogue.* <note>The Ten Commandments</note>

God certainly considers His own children to be free, but not so as to be unrestrained; and so He has bounded a sacred enclosure with certain limits, ten of which I have noted. These are of such great equity, and in such harmony with **Nature**, that nothing more reasonable could have been given; while in the meantime the laws of the **World** demand all manner of unworthy and most absurd things. Provision is therefore made so that no Christian should acknowledge any but his one **God**, since He is able to stand in the place of all things, and mercifully wishes to do so: that no one should attack or assault **God** with his tongue: that no one should deny or disturb the rest assigned to the soul <note>A reference to the Sabbath as a spiritual rest</note>: that no one should defraud his **Parents** of their due: that no one should shed human blood: that no one should dissolve the bond of marriage or pollute its purity: that no one...

The author begins a summary of the commandments, emphasizing their role in protecting the relationship with God, the family, and the sanctity of life and marriage.</summary>

<keywords>Decalogue, Ten Commandments, Christian freedom, divine law, natural law, worldly laws, spiritual rest, parental honor, sanctity of marriage</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the Ten Commandments from the previous page, specifically addressing the prohibitions against theft, false witness, and coveting, before transitioning into a philosophical discussion on the essence of the Law.</meta>

->THE CHRISTIAN.<-

[that no one] should possess what belongs to another: that he should not obstruct the truth or do harm with falsehood: that he should not covet the roof of another: that he should not plot against the household goods of his neighbor. Within these boundaries is enclosed the fellowship of the good, the briefest summary of which is: *To be joined to God, and to wish the best for one's brother*, in which all Religion <term>Religio: spiritual duty and piety</term> and Civil Order <term>Politia: the governance and conduct of a community</term> consist, and by which they are most happily administered. For he who loves God with all his heart, with all his soul, and with all his strength, has no need to be instructed on how and how often he should honor, adore, and worship God; rather, this very union <term>unio: spiritual oneness with the divine</term> and most ready obedience is a burnt offering <term>holocaustum: a complete sacrifice, traditionally one entirely consumed by fire</term> most pleasing to God; this very resignation of the soul <term>animae resignatio: the act of surrendering one's will to God</term> is a holy sacrifice. Likewise, he who loves his brother as himself needs nothing of political precepts <term>Politicorum praecepta: legal or social rules</term> to know how not to cause harm and how to give each his due; instead, the inclination of the mind and the dictate of love <term>amoris dictamen: the inner guidance provided by love</term> spontaneously suggest all things—and even go beyond all requirements of integrity—concerning what good should be bestowed upon individuals. It is the mark of a malicious man to seek out the law, and to wish to know what is proper from written tablets, when we [have] God [dwelling] in our hearts... <note>The Latin text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "git" combined with "de-" suggests "degit," meaning "dwells" or "lives."</note>

<summary>This page concludes the summary of the Ten Commandments and argues that for the true believer, written laws are superseded by a spontaneous, internal dictate of love for God and neighbor. It presents the surrender of the soul as the highest form of worship, far exceeding mere ritual or legal compliance.</summary>

### CITIZEN

...exhausted, they may be convinced that what we seek for ourselves is likewise owed to others.

However, God does not allow one person to rule over everyone in His kingdom, but has entrusted this arduous task to many. For while it is necessary that the person who presides over all be the best and most perfect of all men—the likes of whom no one ever saw except *Christ*, or ever will see—let the title of Monarch <note>original: "Monarchæ"</note> be left to Christ alone. We, however, being distinct members <note>A reference to the biblical metaphor of the "Body of Christ," where different people serve as different parts of a single body.</note> of the body, should provide for the public good <term>res publica: the "common wealth" or the collective interests of the community</term> with the greatest integrity. 

The world, to be sure, may have its own heads of state, of whatever size and vastness it desires; but here, in this tiny domain, I have found nothing of that sort—partly because one man is not sufficient to care for so many matters, and partly because a man refrains from excess only with the greatest difficulty. It is better, while we have not yet shed this human flesh <term>humanam carnem: the mortal condition and its physical limitations</term>, for one person to be restrained by the authority of another, helped by their strengths, guided by their advice, and supported by their shared labors, than to boast of powers and vigils that exceed one's conscience <term>conscientia: the inner moral sense or self-awareness</term>. Unless, of course, we believe a Christian could truly believe himself equal to this burden, which [would exhaust] a thousand of the most hardworking...

<summary>The author argues against absolute earthly monarchy, asserting that the title of "Monarch" belongs only to Christ. He advocates for a collective form of governance where individuals support and restrain one another to prevent the corruption that comes with absolute power.</summary>

<keywords>monarchy, Christ, public good, commonwealth, checks and balances, human nature, humility, collective governance</keywords>

# CHRISTIAN

Indeed, our human affairs are such that, no matter the level of human excellence, we are forced to acknowledge our own failings. We must settle for that which is "less imperfect" rather than seeking the truly perfect among men. Therefore, it is of the utmost importance that we do not entrust ourselves and everything we own to a single person and submit to them; nor should any one man dare to take the care and protection of everyone else upon himself.

Let us instead worship Christ, so that He may rule us by His word and provide us with distinguished members <note>Continuing the metaphor of the "Body of Christ," where different people serve as different functional parts of a single spiritual body.</note> whom He Himself might guide toward the growth of the Christian commonwealth <term>Christianæ rei: the "Christian thing" or the collective society of believers</term>. # CITIZEN

### XI.
### The One.

It is the will of the one God that while the world delights in a variety of temperaments and opinions, and is composed entirely of contradictions, He Himself proposes a rule of Unity to His own people and aligns everyone to a single standard. I saw, therefore, men consecrated to God measured against a certain standard, so that whatever was in excess or lacking in individuals could be taken away or added <note>This imagery suggests a "divine carpentry" where individuals are shaped and planed to fit perfectly into the spiritual structure of the church.</note>. God delights in unity to such an extent that His own people all feel the same, desire the same, believe the same, and speak the same, because they have all been taught by the *One* spirit. It is a thing to be wondered at: that men who have never seen or heard one another should reflect each other perfectly <note>original: "ad unguem," a Latin idiom meaning "to the fingernail," implying absolute precision or perfection, like a sculptor checking a seam with a thumbnail.</note>, and both sound the same and carry the same "scent," as if they had been born as twins.

*This is the greatest mystery of Christian union and a complete prelude to eternity, where all things will be carried out with one harmonious spirit. Thus, it is one and the same thing that God willed in both of His covenants* <term>fœdere: the formal agreements between God and humanity, specifically the Old and New Testaments</term>*, and between them most exact—* <meta>The text breaks here; the catchword "mè" indicates the next page begins with "exactly" (exactissimè).</meta>

<margin>Unity, Christian, mystery, eternity, covenant, God</margin>

<summary>This page explores the concept of spiritual unity, arguing that God imposes a singular standard of belief and character upon his followers to harmonize the natural contradictions of the human world. This "oneness" among believers is presented as a miraculous reflection of the Holy Spirit and a preview of the harmony of heaven.</summary>

<keywords>unity, Holy Spirit, divine standard, Christian mystery, covenants, spiritual harmony, conformity to God</keywords>

...exactly consistent: One Messiah <note>original: "Messias"</note> has obtained life and the inheritance of heaven for all who are to be saved. There is also one and the same drama <note>original: "actio," a term referring to the movement or performance of a play</note> of this world, which, although it changes its characters and scenery in various ways, always returns—and, to your surprise, with a triple face. Yet God has this unique way: just as He commands the sun’s light to rise and set, so He wills His own works to shine with a light now dimmer, now brighter; He wills them to rise and to fall. Just as no one can stop these cycles, so hardly anyone can look into them unless they have been taught by God. 

Because of this, many try in vain to drive away the darkness, and many also try to block the morning radiance <note>original: "jubar," the first light of the sun or a radiant beam</note>; they perish in the midst of their daring attempts. Only those are wise who keep their affairs united with God. He alone has the right to lead His people out and bring them back; for us, it is most beneficial to follow at His nod.

It was right to wonder why there is less of a battle over the truth here than in the World: for many people are entirely convinced that much is being given to God if we argue fiercely about Him among ourselves, and with much reasoning—

Indeed, it is such that as many of us Christians as inhabit this earth ought to behave as members of a single body, devoted to the health, sufficiency, and care of everyone. It is never tolerable that a few should have abundance while the many waste away; that a few should stand out while the many are trampled; that a few should be wise while the many are driven to folly; or that a few should enjoy leisure while the many are forced to toil.

What sort of proportion is this among Christians? What rationality <note>original: "rationabilitas," the quality of being guided by reason and fairness</note>? That one man is born to every luxury and wastes his life in these things without any public benefit, while another cannot rise up even through his own merits and hard work! That for one man every evil is permitted, but for the other, not even the doing of good is allowed? Is this not barbarism rather than Christianity? For Christianity persuades us to dispense earthly things in such a way...

Equality existed alongside superiority; superiority alongside equality. Honor was everywhere voluntary, and the will itself was honorable. This unity can be the most learned, the most wealthy, and the strongest. ### XIII.
### *Blessedness.*

Time reminds us that we should now explain the innermost sanctuary of the sacred shrine itself <note>original: "sacrifanii penetrale," a metaphor for the heart of the Christian mystery</note>; this is the <term>Highest Good <note>original: "summum bonum"; the ultimate goal of human existence</note></term>. This is not that philosophical dream—which one might nevertheless rightly call the peak of human reason—but rather the embrace of Divine grace and the possession of internal blessedness. Christ, the most wise interpreter, called this the **Kingdom of Heaven** <note>original: "Regnum Cœlorum"</note>, when the human breast carries within itself the infinite treasures of Divine goodness, enclosed and deposited there, which to seek out and unfold is a source of ineffable pleasure. For God has entrusted to the devoted heart an epitome of Heaven, which is finally the true <term>Self-Sufficiency <note>original Greek: "Autarcia" (αὐτάρκεια); the virtue of being content and needing nothing external</note></term>, whose possessors find rest in the whole <note>original: "in solidum," a legal term implying a full and undivided interest</note> and can never be in need. Wherefore some have squandered all their belongings for this **pearl** <note>A reference to the "pearl of great price" from the Gospel of Matthew</note>, ready to give up a world monarchy with the same ease if God required it; others, made rich by this union, even [surrendered] their bodies...

<vocab>Blessedness, Kingdom of Heaven, Self-Sufficiency, Divine goodness, highest good, Christ</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the "Highest Good" not as a philosophical concept, but as the internal possession of Divine grace and the "Kingdom of Heaven" within the heart. It describes this state as a true "Autarcia" or self-sufficiency that outweighs all worldly wealth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of the "Highest Good" or internal blessedness of the Christian. It completes the sentence from the previous page regarding those who have renounced worldly goods for the "pearl" of divine grace.</meta>

->CITIZEN<-

...thrown away everything, ready to undergo a thousand deaths with the same readiness if the World should inflict them. Others, blessed by this gem, have forgotten their own selves, **returned to nothing** <note>original: "in nihilum redierunt," likely referring to the mystical practice of self-annihilation or "kenosis," where the ego is emptied to be filled by God</note>, and immersed themselves in the Divinity. 

This is that sweetness which, whoever tastes it, can no longer be affected by any pleasure, nor cast down by any sorrow; he cannot be captured by any charms, nor terrified by any threats, nor raised up by any honors, nor afflicted by any punishments. He cannot be delighted by any life, nor tormented by any death; but everywhere he has enough within himself, anxious about no other thing. Whether the World goes up or down, or even collapses into itself, this man says that none of it pertains to him, since as long as Heaven is safe, he holds all his possessions safe. 

This is a most happy wealth indeed, which no force can overcome and no trickery can subvert. By an admirable transformation, it contracts all things of the Universe into a single point, and from that point draws them out into the immense, so that it may be more truly said of no one that he is less **alone** than a Christian when he is alone—being, of course, accompanied by Heaven.

Not absurdly did Plato once hold the opinion that **all things lie hidden within man**;

From this well, one may draw inexhaustible waters of arts, sciences, inventions, and meditations, and abundantly pour them out for the Christian cause. 

This vital fountain leaves the World astonished, as it sees more and more mysteries and the **great works of God** <note>original: "magnalia Dei," a common biblical term for God's miraculous deeds</note> flow forth. The World sees that such a rich vein never runs dry, even though the World’s own sea of vanities contributes nothing to it. For it is, in truth, a kind of poverty to borrow everything from external sights and the noisy voices of the crowd, drawing nothing from the treasure of one's own heart—even though nothing is more common in the World than this spiritual destitution. You may see begged-for examples and hear words stitched together from others; even when they have achieved much, the work remains someone else’s...

->*The Comforter.*<-

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 50, completing the word "axiomata" (axioms). It transitions from the critique of worldly "pre-chewed" knowledge to the necessity of the Holy Spirit's direct illumination.</meta>

# THE CHRISTIAN

...axioms and demonstrations. Here there are no tiny lamps, but a sacred flame that illuminates everything. There are no tinted spectacles here, but eyes that carry the rays of the sun and penetrate all solid things. Here, indeed, there is need for a **clean slate** <term>rasa tabula</term> <note>original: "rasa tabula," referring to a wax tablet that has been scraped clean; a mind free of worldly preconceptions</note> which bears no characters of the World, no smears or letters of the earth, and no marks of Satan, but is instead purified by a sacred cleansing and whitened with a divine brightness. Such a slate receives the words of the **Supreme Dictator** <note>original: "Dictatoris," here referring to God as the ultimate authority and author of truth</note> and gives them back whenever there is need. 

The **World** is utterly ignorant of this **School of Pentecost**, yet it nonetheless boasts of various "spiritual" matters and takes upon itself the interpretation of all God’s hidden secrets. How skillfully it does so, it must see for itself! Certainly, anyone who has been educated only in the school of the World brings words that are too cold, senses that are too dull, and weaknesses that are too great to explain God. **That sacred hearth must be present**, by which a person may grow warm and bring forth unusual things—and even glowing coals from the altar of God <note>A reference to Isaiah 6:6, where a coal from the altar purifies the prophet's lips</note>—if anything beyond the common fancies of the crowd is to be communicated to the Church. Because this capacity belongs to only a few, let us not wonder that we are less like **prophets** <note>original: "fatidici," literally "fate-speakers" or those who announce divine truth</note>...

Then we shall know perfectly what God willed by every **jot and tittle** <term>apicibus</term> <note>original: "singulis apicibus," referring to the smallest strokes of a pen or the tiny horns on Hebrew letters; here meaning God's intent in even the smallest details of his word</note> through Himself, and not through any other **Master** <note>original: "Magistro"</note>.

The disciple of this teacher is our **Conscience** <note>original: "Conscientia"</note>, which stands forth by a silent consent like a thousand witnesses. While other men treat this conscience with neglect, the Christian observes it most tenderly, and listens even when it is only muttering. For all our internal affairs are such that they reveal themselves to us with great frankness, no matter how much we might try to hide them behind an external facade or a lying voice. Our **weakness**, **ignorance**, **poverty**, and **emptiness**—or, in a single word, **Vanity** <note>original: "Vanitas"</note>—are clear to us, and yet we dare to deny this not so much to others as to ourselves. Indeed, even impiety is ashamed of itself while conscience reproaches it; impiety unhappily looks out for itself with this one skill: trying to lull the **barking conscience** <note>original: "latrantem conscientiam"; a vivid historical metaphor for the persistent, noisy reminder of guilt or moral duty</note> to sleep. # THE CHRISTIAN <note>original: "CHRISTIANUS"</note>

It is blessedly provided for when the **spirit** <note>original: "spiritus"</note> possesses a **teachable conscience** <note>original: "conscientiam docilem"; a conscience that is ready and willing to be instructed by God</note> which clearly sees and confesses that God's commands are to be desired and are beneficial to the soul, regardless of whatever an opposing tongue may **prattle** <note>original: "cogganniat"; literally to growl or mutter like a dog, used here to describe the distracting voices of worldly gossip or internal doubt</note> against them. From this, the soul takes heart to obey God, even while the **flesh** <note>original: "carne"</note> sets itself in opposition.

Indeed, one must dare to do that which the spirit has already proven to you to be true. One must not hesitate while that internal testimony of truth and goodness withers away through various excuses and delays. That which the soul has conceived under the guidance of the spirit must be attempted, lest it rot beneath the **membranes of the body** <note>original: "corporis membranis"; a vivid anatomical metaphor suggesting that spiritual insights must be "born" into action or they will decay inside the physical self</note>. That which is established by divine commands and heavenly logic must be expressed, lest the opportunity of the moment vanish while we are still deliberating and examining it.

For if ever there was harm or guilt in losing an opportunity, or if it was ever foolish to lose a desired thing through delay, then in **Divine matters**—once they have been admitted and approved by our very Conscience—all **procrastination** <term>procrastinatio</term> <note>the act of putting things off until tomorrow (cras)</note> is most vicious and

### Justice. <note>original: "Justitia"</note>

No one has ever served God for nothing; everything is full of rewards and heavenly gifts. And yet, many are convinced that labor spent on God is wasted, since it contributes nothing toward the fame, honors, and salaries <note>original: "salaria"; specifically referring to worldly compensation or fixed pay</note> of this world. This is surely a very great error; for if they could perceive how generously God treats His own, and how He blesses each person with nearly unique and singular gifts, they would prefer to receive the hidden rewards <note>original: "secreta"; literally 'secrets' or 'private things,' implying internal spiritual peace</note> of God rather than the **noisy wages** <note>original: "clamosa stipendia"; 'stipendia' often refers to military pay, suggesting the boisterous, public nature of worldly recognition</note> of the world. 

For who would not believe that he who can command his own desires is superior to the one thirsting for honor or hungering for gold? And who would not see that the one who embraces all things through **simple faith** <note>original: "fide simplici"</note> is better than the inquisitive doubter <note>original: "curioso dubitatori"; 'curiosus' here implies a meddlesome or overly skeptical mindset that seeks to test everything rather than trust</note>? Or that he who settles his adversaries with kindness surpasses the conqueror and the victor? We could say the same of other **virtues** <note>original: "virtutibus"</note>: there is not one among them whose value does not exceed the outward appearance and false pleasure of all **vices** <note>original: "vitiorum"</note>. To the most just... <note>The text ends mid-sentence, transitioning to the next page's focus on God as the 'most just' judge.</note>

Therefore, whatever God has enjoined upon us, we should perform not so much because we are compelled, but because we are most willing.

They are indeed most rigid toward themselves, because in searching the depths of the heart <note>original: "ima cordis"</note> they find everything full of infection and corruption; they discover everything to be painted over and encrusted <note>original: "fucata & incrustata"; referring to something disguised or falsely adorned</note>, and everything subject to some passion. # THE CHRISTIAN <note>original: "CHRISTIANUS"</note>

## XVII.

### *Movement.* <note>original: "Motus"; this can refer to physical motion, the "stirrings" of the soul, or purposeful activity.</note>

Just as the World tortures <note>original: "excarnificat"; literally "to strip the flesh from the bone"</note> its followers with insane labors, so God exercises His own with noble <note>original: "liberalibus"; suggesting activities that are honorable and befitting a free person rather than a slave</note> movements. For such is the nature of idleness <note>original: "otij"</note> that it corrodes others, but truly makes Christians rot. For as soon as a Christian evades the labors assigned by God, he swarms with worms and other insects, by which he is consumed in a foul manner. Therefore, the supreme commander has ordered that each individual be occupied in some good duty <note>original: "officio"</note>, which, when joined with others, constitutes the Christian commonwealth <note>original: "rempublicam Christianam"</note>. Let some therefore teach, others rule, others counsel, others instruct, others discover, and others judge; yet let all labor, and let them come together in that which leads to the journey toward the Heavens. For he who wishes to labor for the world has lost both his effort and his time. The world treats no one more harshly than the person who has spent his entire life in its service. That movement is best which strives upward from the earth to heaven, and returns to the source of perfection, which fir- <note>The text breaks off at "pri-"; the catchword "mus" indicates the word is "primus," meaning "first" or "the first [movement]."</note>

We must even eat and drink as Christians; to such an extent does Christ not approve of or tolerate foreign rituals and customs among his own people. He who neglects this has not yet felt the heavenly movement within himself, which, being fiery in its nature, calls him to higher things. Meanwhile, the man burdened by earthly gravity sinks downward, and even if lifted up a thousand times, he returns to the depths. There is, you see, as much difference between a Christian and a Worldly Man <note>original: "Mundanum"</note> as there is between a released ***stone and a bird.***

# CHRISTIAN.

...by a common sense, so it calls [everyone] together through a communion of help. On the contrary, the true mark of the **World** is to delight in the misfortunes of others, to mock the errors of others, and to grow rich from the losses of others. Every worst person laughs at the blemishes <term>nævos: literally birthmarks or moles; used here to mean small moral faults or defects</term> of the good, and he who is the most corrupt of all corrects others. 

It is otherwise for the **Christian**, who is intimately moved by the misfortunes of his brother, and avoids and turns them away as effectively as he can; but if he cannot entirely hinder the fury of the World, he testifies to his solidarity through public mourning. For whatever anywhere acknowledges Christ and suffers for that reason belongs to us, and touches the "public cause," which is believed to suffer even in its smallest member. 

Therefore, no one shall call himself an "abounding" Christian while his brother is in need; no Christian would dare to rest while his brother is in agony. The business of all is at stake when it is the business of one, for those who are in Christ ought to be a single soul. 

But look, I pray, at those sham <note>original: "fictitios"; refers to nominal Christians who are Christian in name only, lacking the true spirit of the faith</note> Christians, how anxiously they tend to their own affairs, while most carelessly neglecting those of others: for from whatever part the scourge of God <term>Dei plaga: a "strike" or "blow" from God, often referring to trials, discipline, or divine punishment</term> tests His own people, they turn themselves away from it, and as if [separated] by a wall inter-

### THE CITIZEN XIX. Charity.

We were all born under this law: that as children of the same Father, we should embrace one another with brotherly love. However, as soon as **Sin** scattered and tore us apart, *Charity* withdrew from the world; it was not brought back to us from heaven except through **Christ**, and only the "born again" <note>original: "renati"; a theological term for those who have undergone spiritual rebirth or regeneration.</note> have received it.

It is to Charity that we owe the fact that the world has not yet fallen into ruin, and that some tolerable form of social interaction <term>commercium (social and civil intercourse)</term> remains. For if we were left to the mercy of human hatreds, who would escape those who wickedly wish for the people of the whole world to have but a single neck? <note>This is an allusion to the Roman Emperor Caligula, who famously wished that the Roman people had only one neck so that he could behead them all at once.</note> 

But **God** willed that there be many hands to resist these beasts—not only among all those who possess reason, but even more so among **Christians**, because He has both commanded and entrusted to them the symbol of mutual Charity. Therefore, as each person either has or does not have charity, he proves himself to be either a Christian or a profane man; indeed, the more charity he possesses, the more he shows himself to be a Christian. For since [it is] most easy <meta>catchword: mum; the word is "facillimum," meaning "most easy"</meta>

It presents Charity as the definitive evidence of true Christian faith and the primary defense against the "beastly" impulses of tyrants.</summary>

# XXI.
## *Liberty.*

**R**ightly indeed has it been said that *each man is held back by his own folly.* For since God not only created us *free*, but *freed* us <note>original: "liberavit"; a play on words between being created as "liberos" (free people) and being "liberavit" (delivered/liberated) through the atonement of Christ.</note> once again through His Son, nothing can seem quite so absurd as the fact that we return again into chains and shackles. For those which are the customs and manners of the World—though they do nothing other than invite us—we believe to be laws, and even a necessity for obeying. From this, very few indeed break these thin threads <note>original: "tenuia fila"; a metaphor for the seemingly light but persistent pressures of social expectation.</note> by which—contrary to their own mind, contrary to conscience, and even contrary to evident advantage—they are bound by their lineage, social status, wife, relatives, friends, and possessions. Thus, they conform themselves less to God and His more perfect rules, but instead drag out the time and [wait for] opportunities

For a Christian prefers to rightly **mold** and **shape** <note>original: "informare & efformare"; educational terms describing the process of character building or "forming" a soul.</note> a single person committed to his care, according to the standard of heaven, rather than be a governor over entire provinces and be found unequal to the weight of human iniquity. While others may demand **prefectures** <note>original: "Præfecturas"; high-ranking administrative or military offices.</note> and whole **administrations** <note>original: "Oeconomias"; in this context, the management of entire estates or districts.</note> for themselves, a Christian man is not ashamed to prove his faith in small things, and even to be cast down in the house of the Lord and to stand at the doors. Let it be enough to be a **servant of God**; for if this is not enough for someone, nothing on earth will ever satisfy them. Let the care for the **Kingdom of God** <note>original: "Regni Dei"</note> not seem base to anyone. For those favored by fortune, who withdraw from this care and judge the noblemen of this earth to be beneath them, testify by this very act that they—though they may be called God’s ministers—are held unworthy by the supreme King. But even those nourished by the **Church**, <note>original: "Ecclesia"</note> who, once they have been fattened, proudly cast off their **divine duties** <note>original: "Divina officia"</note> onto the hungry and keep the fat of the burnt offerings <note>A biblical allusion to the corrupt sons of Eli in 1 Samuel, who took the best parts of the sacrifices for themselves.</note> for themselves, shall not escape the supreme deity.

### XXII.
#### *Resignation* <note>original: "Resignatio"; here meaning the voluntary renunciation or surrender of worldly claims and possessions.</note>

There was a time when it was permitted to dominate this Earth, and possessing it was a joy; but now, it is most wise to renounce it and to participate in nothing of it at all. For as soon as it was infected by the **corruption** <note>original: "sanie"; literally the discharge or "pus" from a wound, used here to describe the moral rot of the world.</note> of **Sin**, <note>original: "Peccatum"</note> he who has the least of it is the most blessed...

<meta>The catchword "mus" at the bottom of the original page indicates the first syllable of the next page's word, "beatissimus" (most blessed).</meta>

<summary>This page argues that true Christian service involves humility in "small things" and warns that those who use the Church for personal gain will face divine judgment. It concludes by introducing the virtue of "Resignation," or the renunciation of a world corrupted by sin.</summary>

<keywords>humility, service, Church corruption, resignation, renunciation, sin, divine judgment, administration</keywords>

...wretched it is. For many are accustomed to seeking not only small fields or their own plot of land, but entire **regions** <note>original: "climata"; referring to large geographical zones or latitudes of the earth.</note>, and to bound their territories by the sun’s daily journey. These are unhappy men, whom a **seven-foot ditch** <note>original: "septempedalis fossa"; a traditional Latin expression for a grave, emphasizing that despite a man's greed for land, he ultimately occupies only enough for a burial.</note> hides and, at last, satisfies.

But Christians, who have surrendered themselves entirely to Christ, *resign* <note>original: "resignānt"; to formally hand back or renounce a claim.</note> into their Savior’s hands everything they could claim here by any right, being wealthy enough if they know they possess nothing apart from God and without God. Hence they judge it a small thing if they only return external possessions to the Lord’s hands, unless they also leave their innermost being to the divine will.

Nothing unexpected can happen to such people, since they count lashings, prisons, punishments, and other things horrible to the World among their **blessings** <note>original: "beneficia"; literally "favors" or "benefits".</note>. To them, prosperity is as much to be feared as adversity seems safe; they delight in the memory of their hardships and glory uniquely in their **welts and scars** <note>original: "plagis & vibicibus".</note>. They have become so **toughened** <note>original: "occalluerunt"; literally "to have grown a thick skin" or become calloused, here meaning spiritually resilient to physical pain.</note> that unless they are suffering, they believe they are doing nothing here and wasting their days. This is a **rare bird** <note>original: "rara avis"; a famous Latin idiom for something exceptionally uncommon or unique.</note>, yet nonetheless sometimes seen. But let anyone refrain their hand—

The occasion prompts us to also contemplate the **crown of the Martyrs**, which is rarely placed upon the great men of this earth, but more frequently upon its **refuse** <note>original: "rejectamentis"; those cast out or rejected by society.</note>. O, the **secret judgments of God** <note>original: "arcana Dei judicia"</note>, which are accustomed to decreeing extreme punishments for His dearest friends in exchange for their most upright deeds and most praiseworthy merits—punishments burdened and defiled by infamous accusations. ...strive for with such **provocation**! <note>original: "irritatione"; this completes the word from the previous page's catchword. What can you snatch away from a man for whom all earthly things are like **filth** <note>original: "cœni"; literally "mud" or "mire," a common term in devotional literature for the worthlessness of material goods compared to the divine.</note>, and bring nothing but trouble? 

To such a person, this entire universe is a place of **exile**, so that it matters little where he is banished. Lest he should let anything go with regret, he has learned to do without all things. He fears nothing—not even that he might not be exalted—since he casts himself down of his own accord. And why, finally, should he avoid the enmities of the World, for whom the **executioner** <note>original: "carnifex"</note> is a friend, and a longed-for one? Give us also, O God, this spirit, and free us from all fear; You who allow those whom You sincerely love to be convicted of wrongdoing here by many witnesses and strict judges, so that when they have testified their faith to You here, they may hear from You Yourself the **testimony of their integrity**.

# THE CITIZEN <note>original: "CIVIS"</note> XXIII. Elevation <note>original: "Elevatio"; refers to the lifting up of the soul toward divine things.</note>

It is most fitting for a person whom God has received among His **household** <note>original: "familiares"; those who are intimate with or belong to the family of God.</note> to value the favor of this earth as nothing. For even if the **World** <note>original: "Mundus"</note> should provide all that it promises to its followers—which is something that happens only very rarely—these things are of little value when compared to those which God is accustomed to share. 

The world is used to promising an **immortality of name** <note>original: "nominis immortalitatem"; meaning posthumous fame or reputation.</note> to its own, employing various structures for that end; as if it were truly the part of death to give life, or of time to promise **eternity** <note>original: "æternitatem"</note>. Even when it does its utmost, it only delays the forgetting of a name for a few years, and does so in a way so obscure, so doubtful, so limited, and with such envy and resistance from many, that it is not worth purchasing such **folly** <note>original: "ineptiam"; a trifle or absurdity.</note> with such exhausting labors. 

But those whom God selects from among men to make the most illustrious among His faithful, He first fills with **wondrous gifts** <note>original: "admirandis donis"</note>, so that **Nature** <note>original: "Natura"</note> itself stands amazed by them. Then He instructs and refines them through **intimate conversations** <note>original: "familiaribus colloquiis"</note> about all matters, so that now no longer...

...conversations, so that they are heard as witnesses not only of the earth, but also of heaven, and are honored like earthly **divinities** <note>original: "Numina"; divine powers or spirits, here used metaphorically for those who reflect God's power on earth.</note>. Indeed, the lot of Christians is different from that of **Worldly people** <note>original: "Mundanis"</note>, as they do not become illustrious unless they are humble; they do not become wise unless they are simple; they overcome nothing unless they are weak; while the World elevates only the proud, the clever, and the strong. 

But the more these worldly ones are lifted up, the more heavily and miserably they fall back down; for only those sit upon a secure height who have been raised up to God by God Himself.

### XXIV.

#### *Prayers.*

This **familiarity with God** <note>original: "Dei familiaritas"</note>, although it depends most purely upon His choice and selection, nonetheless consists of many and perpetual *prayers*. For there is one method: to have God responding, one must question Him; to have Him speaking, one must address Him; to have Him appearing, one must invite Him; to have Him consenting, one must entreat Him; and to have Him running alongside, one must implore Him. Through this *striving* <note>original: "ambitu"; here referring to the persistent effort or "circuit" of spiritual seeking to gain favor</note> the friends of God [insinuate themselves] <note>Completed by the catchword "runt," indicating they have introduced themselves into God's presence.</note>

<summary>The author explains that only those spiritually attuned to God can understand His secret language and symbols, and identifies constant prayer as the essential method for maintaining this divine intimacy.</summary>
