## XXV.

->*Wholeness*<-
<note>original: "Rotunditas"; literally "roundness." In classical and Renaissance thought, a "round" person was someone whose character was a perfect sphere—smooth, self-contained, and unshaken by external fortune.</note>

The **World** <note>original: "Mundus"</note> *believes it brings back outstanding praise and rich spoils whenever it devises a snare. For since all its inhabitants are of a foxy nature—and it is very difficult to deceive a fox—the World finds no greater pleasure than when it actually catches someone. While, therefore, one craft eludes another,* the **Christian** <note>original: "Christianus"</note> *is secure in his own wholeness. For in the manner of a **hedgehog** <term>hedgehog<gloss>original: "Echini"; the author uses the hedgehog as a symbol of the soul that "rolls into a ball" to protect itself from worldly influence</gloss></term>, he seeks no hiding places or winding passages, but returns into himself. And no matter which way he is rolled, he is not harmed. There are also spines belonging to the* **Christian**, *which the tender hands of the* **World** *cannot bear, and which the mouths of dogs are less likely to snatch. Whether **Hypocrisy** <note>original: "Hypocrisis"</note> lies in wait for tyranny, or tyranny subjugates hypocrisy, or sophistry circumvents them both, or both of them whip sophistry; while others are anxiously afflicted,* the **Christian** *despises this game of **Satan** <note>original: "Satanæ"</note>, certain that nothing good is to be expected from the* **World**, *and that it matters little who at last gains power over affairs. For these are [conflicts] of demons among themselves*

<meta>catchword: with</meta>

<summary>This page describes the Christian's "wholeness" or "roundness" as a spiritual defense against the deceptions and power struggles of the world. Using the metaphor of a hedgehog, the author argues that a true believer remains unharmed by worldly traps because they find security within their own integrity.</summary>
<keywords>wholeness, integrity, worldliness, hedgehog metaphor, spiritual resilience, hypocrisy, tyranny, Satan's games</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Christian's relationship to worldly power and conflict. The previous page ended by describing worldly struggles as "conflicts of demons among themselves," and this page elaborates on why a believer should not join these factions.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>THE CITIZEN</header>

conflicts, which on both sides consist of trickery and violence, and never have anything truly in common with **Christ** <note>original: "Christo"</note>. For although they very often hold out "public safety," "religion," "integrity," "liberty," and beautiful names of that kind as a pretext, yet if anyone looks beneath the skin <note>original: "intra cuticulam"; a phrase borrowed from the Roman satirist Persius, meaning to look at the hidden reality or true character beneath a surface appearance</note> at those administrators of the *public good* <note>original: "boni publici"</note>, he will see that the kingdom is being prepared for themselves, not for **Christ**, and that they are not worthy for a **godly man** <note>original: "vir pius"</note> to join his cause with theirs.

But the **house of God** <note>original: "domus Dei"</note> will stand unshaken, and it will blunt all hostile blows, regardless of how many people withdraw their hands or how many refuse to defend it. For it was not built by human hands, nor has it been defended by human help up to this day; rather, God has commanded that what is right remain right for all eternity, and all those who are upright in heart follow it.

It is of great importance to know what befits a good man *in troubled times* <note>original: "rebus turbatis"; refers to periods of civil unrest or political upheaval</note>; for some rise up too rashly, while others flee too timidly. This concern has troubled even moderate minds, when they saw what they were fleeing, but did not see where they were fleeing to.

<meta>catchword: ...to see [vident]</meta>

Now, if one side grows powerful and rises above the other, no moderation can be hoped for, nor can any remedy of balance be expected. Let the wounds of the **Church** <term>Ecclesia: the universal body of Christian believers</term> be a proof to us, gaping as they are, torn apart by such bitter hatreds. What should he do, who is displeased by the vehemence of opposing factions, and who does not approve of the blind impulse on both sides—or rather, their open dissolution! For if he withdraws himself, he is accused of weakness; if he follows along even slowly, he is accused of **complicity** <note>original: "consensus"</note>; and if he disapproves of anything, he is guilty of desertion. 

But let us say, entirely with God’s guidance, that a good man, in a *bad* <note>original: "malo"</note> time, must testify to his dissent most modestly, and offer suitable remedies if he can. Every best man has done this, receiving little favor for a time and much hatred, but with posterity eventually admitting and honoring his efforts. How few of those grumblers live on in the records of **good memory** <note>original: "tabulis bonæ memoriæ"; referring to historical reputation</note>—far be it that they should ever be read in the **book of life** <note>original: "libro vitæ"; a biblical concept referring to those destined for salvation</note>. But if those are blessed and honored in the world, who for their **Fatherland** <note>original: "Patria"</note> die—

<summary>The author describes the impossible position of the moderate person during times of factional conflict, where neutrality is mistaken for cowardice or betrayal. He argues that the truly good man must quietly maintain his integrity and offer solutions, seeking approval from history and God rather than from partisan crowds.</summary>

### XXVI.
### *Tolerance.*

The royal road of living is to **tolerate** <note>original: "tolerare"</note> what you cannot correct; there is hardly a more dangerous detour than impatience. For while we struggle in vain against things, that which would still be tolerable becomes bitter, and is further aggravated by an irritated World. Many who try to unburden themselves only take up greater weights upon their shoulders, by which they are crushed. Far wiser than these are the Christians who, following their Master’s **paradox** <note>original: "paradoxo"; referring to the counter-intuitive teachings of Jesus in the Sermon on the Mount</note>, turn the other cheek to the one who delivers a **blow** <term>colaphum: a strike with the fist or hand</term> to the right side, and to the one taking their tunic, they also grant...

The **World** <term>Mundus: the secular or material realm, often contrasted with the spiritual life</term> should never seem so great to us that we cannot bear it, nor ever so precious that we cannot do without it. Whether the World wishes to oppress or to restrain, let it not be a burden to the **Christian** <note>original: "Christiano"</note>, who can be anywhere except away from **Christ** <note>original: "Christo"</note>, and can endure all things except the wrath of God.

Many things must displease a good man, but they should not make his spirit sick. Let those things perish which admit no salvation. Let those things go backward which refuse to progress; let those things slip away which cannot be lifted up. Why should the Christian torture himself amidst these things, he who has satisfied his **Conscience** <term>Conscientia: the internal moral compass or the soul's witness before God</term> and has more than enough of the cross of Christ? 

Men are unwilling to live according to our character; let us live according to theirs, if the righteousness of our soul allows it. The **Age** <term>Seculum: the current era of human history, often perceived by Renaissance and Baroque writers as being in a state of terminal decline</term> rushes toward the worse! Why? Shall we hinder the old age of the World, or postpone its **delirium** <note>original: "delirium"; here implying the irrationality or madness of a world nearing its end</note>? Rather, let us take care that nothing of our own is swept away with it, for those things which are outside our power will stand or fall under another’s command. Let us not even groan when the supreme commander orders...

Certainly, a Christian avoids this way of obtaining justice as much as possible, while the lust for litigation—

## XXVII.
### *Mercy.*

**N**ow that our affairs have been arranged under the guidance of **Justice**, we must examine another path, which we rightly call **Mercy**. For since the former path might appear too harsh and too rigid—demanding as it does such great perfection—it is softened by this second path, as we see that God wishes us well even in our weakness. 

For He reconciled us to Himself through His Son, our Christ, not merely as personal rivals, but as **sworn enemies** <note>original: "hostes"; in Latin, "inimicus" is a personal foe, whereas "hostis" is a public enemy or a foreigner at war, implying a deeper state of rebellion</note>. He raised us up when we were not merely sleeping, but dead; He healed us when we were not merely ill, but hopeless; He cleansed us when we were not merely stained, but rotting. 

Thus, the impulse of Mercy must be infinite, for by it He sustains us even now—though we remain strangers, indifferent, fainting, and foul-smelling—and covers us with the wings of His clemency. Therefore, He gathers the **Church** <term>Ecclesia: the community of believers</term> to Himself not only from the wise, the prudent, the strong, and the temperate, but He also includes many from the crowd of sinners. In this way, He shows Himself to be the ultimate **Physician**, who [treats] epidem-

### XXVIII.
### *Forgiveness* <term>Remissio: the release from a debt or the pardon of sins</term>

Behold, O man, what you owe to your God, with whom you have been reconciled in an inexpressible way, and whose mercies are above all His works. You are encouraged by the **tax collectors** <term>publicani: officials who collected taxes for Rome, often despised as traitors or sinners in the New Testament</term>, **prostitutes**, and **thieves**, who, having been snatched in a moment from the jaws of the enemy, are received into the lands of the **Shepherd** <note>A reference to Christ as the "Good Shepherd" who seeks the lost</note>. You provoked God, yet He pacifies you; you stirred His fury, yet He settles you; you sinned, yet He suffered; you failed, yet He still endures you. If you repent, He spares you; if you return, He receives you; if you delay, He waits for you. He calls back the wanderer, invites the resistant, waits for the sluggish, and embraces the one who returns. He teaches...

[Rest] is obtained; for while nature favors those who act, grace <note>original: *haec* (this), referring back to the "Rest" or "Grace" introduced in the previous section</note> requires those who are patient. By moving and running about, a person advances nothing in the **divine light** <term>divino lumine: the spiritual insight or presence of God that illuminates the soul</term>, but rather by sitting at the feet of the Lord and listening. He sits, however, who waits for God and is not troubled by the turmoils of the world. 

God does not always communicate what He wants or when He wants it, nor does He reveal to His people where He leads; rather, He walks with His beloved through winding paths and **mysterious turns** <term>ambages: a Latin term for riddles, delays, or indirect, circuitous routes</term>. Here our curiosity must fall silent and not look around for any shortcuts, since even **spiral lines** <note>The author uses the image of a spiral to suggest that what looks like circling or backtracking to a human observer is actually a direct and "straight" path according to God's geometry.</note> are straight enough for God. 

We very often seem to ourselves to be wandering, and yet we are proceeding most correctly; to be hindered, and yet we move forward; to stand still, and yet we hasten; to step back, and yet we draw near. Many go through **prisons** <term>carceres: can refer to literal imprisonment or figurative states of confinement and trial</term> to reach kingdoms by an unusual route, or through injuries to achieve a good name. 

*Rest, O man, and commit the whole matter to God, who, beyond all your understanding, has already arranged His affairs beforehand and commands them to proceed in the best order.* 

Therefore, a Christian does this one thing: he keeps the **pillar of fire** <note>original: *columnam ignis*; a reference to the Book of Exodus, where God guided the Israelites through the wilderness by a pillar of cloud by day and a pillar of fire by night.</note> always before his eyes, wherever he may go,

### XXX.
### *Silence.*

The guarding of God's blessings under silence is a blessed thing, because, in a manner contrary to this world, it enriches the one who possesses it. For the **World** thinks it knows nothing unless many people are aware of its knowledge; but the **Christian**, if others know of it, considers his treasures to have been betrayed. It is, moreover, truly the **will of God** <term>voluntas Dei: the divine purpose or desire for human conduct</term> that we should be of the most restrained tongue, and not rashly blurt out His secrets: for He cannot endure a **noisy soul** <note>original: *streperam animam*. A soul cluttered with the clamor of self-promotion and external chatter.</note>, but rather one that performs the sacred rites of silence with devotion and faithfulness.

To be sure, the **great works of God** <note>original: *magnalia Dei*. A biblical phrase referring to the wonderful acts or truths of God that should be shared for the benefit of others.</note> are not to be kept silent, nor are those things which can help **Christendom** to be hidden: but there remain other things which God stirs in the ***silent soul***; there are other secrets of **Divine intimacy** <note>original: *familiaritatis Divinæ arcana*. Private spiritual insights or "mystical" experiences intended for the individual's growth rather than public display.</note>, which to publish would be a sign of ambition and levity, rather than a desire for the common good. Indeed, these things immediately desert a talkative man as soon as the **seal of the mouth** is loo- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely from "solvitur" (loosened or broken), leading into the next page.</note>

Certainly, to have someone into whose bosom you may securely and freely pour out everything, even your **sins**, I know not what...

## 31.

### Faith.

The treasure of the **Church** is *Faith* <note>original: *Fides*</note>, which is a certain and singular trust in **Christ** within our hearts. It is kindled by holy promises, and is both sealed and strengthened by the **Holy Spirit** himself and the sacred seals <note>original: *sacris signaculis*. This term historically refers to the sacraments, such as Baptism or the Eucharist, which were seen as outward signs or "seals" of inward grace.</note>. Neither the gates of hell can resist this faith, nor the corruptions of the **World** command it, nor even the weaknesses of the flesh stand in its way to the harm of the **Christian**. This is that root of all goodness which, once implanted through the word of **God**, cannot help but bear good fruit. This is that sacred fire which, kindled by the **Holy Spirit**,

## XXXII.
### *The Word*

The rule of faith is the word of God, which has been expressed for us on the sacred page and communicated by His most merciful counsel. In it, God explains and reveals Himself to man, so that His will and His affection toward us might be most clearly established. Its sum and essence is the Messiah, or Christ, who enters our heart through this wrapping of words <note>original: *verborum involucro*; a metaphor suggesting that the written scripture is the outer vessel or "shroud" that carries the living Christ into the soul</note>, occupying, hallowing, ruling, and protecting it, and lifting it up toward heavenly Wisdom. Its true interpreter and Teacher is the Holy Spirit, who instills its life, vigor <note>original: *nervos*, literally "nerves" or "sinews," implying the underlying strength of the text</note>, powers, mysteries, and light within us, illuminating them with a wondrous fire. Its truest seals <term>signacula</term> <note>In a theological context, these are visible signs or tokens—the sacraments—that represent and seal a spiritual reality.</note> are: the sacred washing <note>original: *lavacrum*; a reference to the Sacrament of Baptism</note>, by which we are cleansed from the filth of our origin <note>Referring to the concept of Original Sin.</note> by heavenly water and, under the guidance of the most Holy Trinity, are adopted into a new covenant of mercy and an order of charity. And the sacred assembly <term>synaxis</term> <note>From the Greek *synaxis*, meaning a gathering or congregation; specifically used here to refer to the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>, by which through the body of Christ and—

<meta>catchword: blood-</meta> <note>original: *sangui-*, the beginning of *sanguine* (blood).</note>

The calamity of these times warns us to candidly inquire into the *Ecclesiastical Calling* <term>Vocatio Ecclesiastica</term> <note>The formal process of appointing a minister. The author is concerned with the breakdown of the traditional three-part selection process involving the clergy, the government, and the congregation.</note>, whether the Church is sufficiently provided for when ancient laws and customs are neglected. By these old ways, it is proper for the bishops <note>original: *sacrorum antistites*; literally "overseers of the sacred things"</note> to call a suitable man, for the magistrate to confirm him, and for the common people to approve him; yet if one or another of these steps is ignored, or trampled by wicked means, can the Church be well-served? No one doubts that this greatly displeases God, burdens the hireling <term>mercenarium</term> <note>A reference to John 10:12; a "mercenary" or "hireling" is a minister who works only for wages rather than spiritual devotion.</note>, and afflicts the house of God <note>original: *domum Dei*</note>. However, not everyone agrees on whether God still acts through such an impure instrument. Those who affirm it say there is some form of calling—though it may be mutilated by the loss of one or two of its parts—and while such a calling should make anyone blush, it nevertheless does not shut God out, nor does it mean the word of God is offered in vain. For divine gifts do not depend on our unworthiness <note>original: *indignitate*</note>; otherwise, the Church would never have enough suit-

If that is not yet the case today, let us not boast; *one goes to destruction by degrees*. Where the Church is valued for some small profit <note>original: *lucello*</note>, and some wealthier part of it stirs up and summons a crowd of "religious" men, one would have to be gullible to believe that these men are moved or held by the business of God. Let us mourn, oh let us mourn our fate, when we see that God hardly wishes to call good men hereafter, but has left the matter to the children of men, so that in another way...

### XXXIII.
### *Contemplation*

We owe immense thanks to our God, who—even though we had cast ourselves down at the feet of Satan through a shameful renunciation of the divine image—did not also snatch away the gift of human reason, but left it as a consolation in our calamity. Although we may abuse it <note>that is, human reason</note> very often, and though many may use it in a manner that is more than irrational, nevertheless, those who have been reformed by Christ are not seduced by it at all; rather, they are further adorned by it under Christian obedience. For God admits the rational man to the contemplation of his wonderful works, so that in this most spacious theater <note>The "Theater of the World" (*theatrum mundi*) was a popular early modern metaphor describing the universe as a stage where God’s glory and wisdom are displayed for humans to observe.</note> he may walk about, all eyes <note>original: *oculeus*; meaning vigilant, observant, or "made of eyes."</note>, and survey the great deeds of God. Light, heaven, earth, stars, sea, animals; and more specifically, the day, the Sun, the Moon, meteors <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and lightning, not just falling stars.</note>, metals, plants, fish, birds, and livestock—these are the "characters" of God <note>original: *characteres*; here meaning distinctive marks, signs, or even letters of a divine alphabet written into nature.</note> by which He instructs us concerning His own clarity, order, power, generosity, and immensity. Through these, He calls us to Himself, so that by taking an example from these creatures, we might provide and exhibit something far more excellent and worthy of our human prerogative. 

He who does not feel these stimuli is utterly senseless; he who is not provoked to follow God by the obedience shown by the creatures is clearly a mere block of wood <note>original: *stipes*; a common Latin term for a dull, unfeeling person, literally a trunk or post.</note>; he who is not set on fire by their readiness is already dead. A Christian, whenever he observes the properties of these things, calls to mind his own proper purpose, which is to worship God. Whenever he sees the fear <note>This likely refers to the instinctive submission or "awe" animals show toward the natural order or their masters.</note> shown by animals, he recognizes his own duty, which is to fear God. Whenever he inspects the fertility of all species, he demands from himself his own fruits, which are to serve God. For he knows that all other things are owed to him, but he alone is owed to God; and he knows that all things are generously bestowed upon us so that we might hesitate less to give ourselves back to God.

### XXXV.
### *Steadfastness.* <note>original: *Soliditas*</note>

No enemy of the Church <note>original: *Ecclesiæ*</note> since the world began <note>original: *ab orbe nato*</note> has been able to fully understand how it happens that the tiny skiff <note>original: *cymba*; a small, fragile boat often used as an allegory for the Church in stormy seas</note> of the Christians cannot be sunk by any waves or most savage storms, nor destroyed or shattered by any engines of war; but though very often torn and pierced, and sometimes even swept away by the waves, it nevertheless survives, and once repaired, continues its voyage. For they did not know that this is her **Steadfastness**, which divine **Omnipotence** has bestowed upon her: that she can overcome and break all adversities, and even more so, conquer through **Patience** <note>original: *Patientia*; in this context, it refers to the power of endurance and suffering under persecution</note>; otherwise, she would have perished more than a thousand times over. And so, when the **tyrants** had exhausted themselves for a long time and spent all the weapons of their fury, finally recognizing the boat as unsinkable, they despaired out of shame and weariness, nor did they leave behind any more successful successors who might renew those ill-fated attempts. For she is always greater and bet—

<summary>This page describes the miraculous resilience of the Church, comparing it to a small, unsinkable boat that survives the violent "storms" of persecution and the fury of tyrants through divine steadfastness.</summary>
<keywords>Church, persecution, steadfastness, endurance, tyrants, allegory of the ship, divine providence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 126 regarding the "Steadfastness" of the Church. The previous page ended with a sentence describing how the Church becomes "always greater and bet—" (melior), which this page completes.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>THE CHRISTIAN.</header>

—ter the Church <note>original: *Ecclesia*</note> rises again, as often as she is thrown down; and while entire empires can be destroyed even to the point of extinction, this small gathering lives through slaughter and grows through destruction. No slaughter is more to be feared than that security in which Christians grow lukewarm, while they burn with fervor during the shedding of blood. No religious peace is more desirable than that which is sanctioned by Divine consent, while earthly peaces are unfaithful and deceptive.

Therefore the Christian boards this vessel <note>original: *ratem*; continuing the nautical metaphor of the Church as a boat from the previous page</note> which he knows can be crushed by no engines of war <note>original: *tormentis*; refers to heavy siege equipment</note>, nor captured by any weapons; thus, it matters very little in what number he undergoes death with his companions. For what he otherwise owes to Nature <note>i.e., his inevitable mortal death</note>, he spends as a praiseworthy profit for the Church, so that he may receive the use and praise even of his own destruction. 

For the field of the Church must be watered with blood <note>original: *Sanguine*</note> so that it may bear more abundant fruit; whoever pours it out is a true <term>Antichrist</term> <note>the ultimate enemy of Christ and the Church</note> and provokes God as an avenger; whoever offers it <note>referring to the martyrs offering their own lives</note> is among the true Christians, and will never go unavenged.

To many it has very often been a miracle that *one* *man* <note>likely referring to the personified "Christian" or the collective body of believers acting as one</note> survives so many dangers and attacks, and having been targeted in so many ways,

## XXXVI.

### *Possession*

The Savior <note>original: *Servator*</note> spoke of this as the "best part" <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke 10:42, where Jesus tells Martha that her sister Mary has chosen the "better part" by listening to him</note>, which can never be taken away. A man eats today only to be eternally hungry; he drinks so that he may be tortured by thirst for all time. Is this what it means to possess something—things which you can lose at any moment, and which you must be without for all eternity?

Not so for the Christian, whose portion <note>original: *pars*</note> is God, and whose share is the joint-inheritance <note>original: *cohæreditas*</note> of eternity. And just as a light kindles infinite other candles without being diminished itself, so this fortunate Merchant <note>original: *Mercator*</note> sets countless others ablaze with heavenly fire. Nothing is taken from his own store; rather, with an unquenchable light, he [fills] both the inner chambers <note>original: *penetralia*; inferred from the catchword "netrale"</note> of his own heart...

Let Christians be ashamed to learn about freedom from **Epictetus**, a man who was once a slave <note>Epictetus (c. 50–135 AD) was a Stoic philosopher who taught that while the body may be enslaved, the mind can remain free. This ensures that our opinions, appetites, desires, distastes, and all our actions remain in our own hands and under our own command, rather than being ruled by the whim of another. Let us firmly fix the idea within ourselves that no earthly thing truly belongs to us; such things can neither help nor harm us, for they pertain only to the body or to external matters.

O man, strike your own wicked mouth if it ever dared to hurl insults against God. Be beaten, wicked tongue, be beaten, be beaten, you who did not blush to attack your **God**; lick up your own saliva with which you spat upon God; swallow back and gulp down the poison that you spewed out against God.

### XXXIX.
#### *Power.* <note>original: *Potentia*.</note>

We have seen the ways of **Justice** and **Mercy**; there remains the path of **Power**, which God shows in His sacred governance, even if His strengths are hidden. However, the nature of this power is entirely different from that of the World; for while the World does nothing except through violence and great lack of restraint <note>original: *impotentia*. While it usually means 'weakness' today, in this context it refers to the world's inability to control its own violent impulses or passions.</note>, God establishes and completes His works with patience. The world acts with pride and puffery <note>original: *tumore*. Literally a 'swelling,' used here to describe bombastic or arrogant behavior.</note>; God handles all things with humility. The world compels and commands those who are most unwilling; God achieves the greatest things through persuasion and rules those who are willing. Hence, it is a mark of the true **Church** that it is not seen here with any outward show of greatness; it is not made formidable by laws or political rules; it is not [sustained] by any force or dece-

**Christ**, on the contrary, subdued the world by suffering. He was not thronged by a band of thieves or cohorts of soldiers, nor was He escorted by a guard of politicians. Instead, He was surrounded by the slaughter of martyrs <note>original: *martyrum strage*</note>—something that no one except the **God-man** <note>original: *homo-Deus*. He never expects Christ to establish some earthly empire where a friend might sit at His right or left hand and give laws to the rest <note>A reference to the request made by the mother of the apostles James and John in Matthew 20:21.</note>. Rather, he knows and has learned already from the records of the Christian empire that he has pledged his name to the **kingdom of the cross** <note>original: *crucis regno*</note>, and that he should expect nothing other than various kinds of afflictions.

### *Miracles.*

The gift of miracles is an evident testimony of Christian powers, granted to those who were to convert the world and destroy idolatry; even now, traces of this remain in those devoted to God.

## XLI.
### *Eternity.*

Among all the things that make the Christian most indifferent <note>original: *negligentem*. In this context, it refers to a holy detachment or a lack of concern for worldly affairs rather than being careless.</note> toward this world, this thought moves him above all others: that all things are temporary, and destined to last for only a small amount of time. This earth possesses nothing stable; all things are in motion, and when they have appeared for a short while, they go away, perish, and *vanish*. In this way, the Empires of the World are born and die, once each has measured out its own small span of time. What, then, should a human being expect for himself—a handful of earth, or rather a tiny speck of dust <note>original: *pulvisculus*. A diminutive of *pulvis* (dust), emphasizing human insignificance in the face of time.</note>—whose greatest age is less than a hundred years? Why should he even be anxious, he to whom the hope of departure is given at every single moment?

Whether something must be done or something must be suffered, it will happen without any lengthy delay. One or another year, day, hour, or minute brings everything to an end. Hence the Christian sees nothing worthy of any anxiety or desire wherever he finds nothing of *eternity*. Honors, wealth, pleasures, fame, and things of this sort pass by like a dream, except for the fact that, for the most part, bit— <note>The text ends mid-word with "ama-", likely intended to be *amaritudinem* (bitterness) or *amari* (bitter), suggesting that worldly pleasures leave a bitter aftertaste.</note>

<vocab>Eternity, Christian, Empires of the world, temporary, tiny speck of dust, world, about to pass away</vocab>

<summary>This page reflects on the transience of human life and worldly power, arguing that a Christian should remain detached from earthly desires because only eternity has lasting value. It emphasizes that since life is short and death is always near, neither worldly success nor suffering is permanent.</summary>

<keywords>eternity, transience, mortality, worldliness, detachment, empires, time</keywords>

bitterness, and leave behind them the shame and weariness of their own emptiness. Therefore, let one be ashamed either to eagerly seek these perishable <note>original: *peritoria*. Refers to things destined to perish or fail.</note> and transitory things, or to let go of them with reluctance. Let it be a shame to buy the noise of a single minute at the cost of so many losses to soul and body. For just as fireworks <note>original: *ignes missiles*. Literally "missile fires," a common 17th-century term for pyrotechnic displays or rockets.</note> are prepared with the labor of many days and insane expense, only to vanish in a tiny moment: so it is for one who devotes his efforts to the follies of the earth, which explode faster than a word can be spoken and vanish into smoke. 

But eternity—eternity is worthy of a noble soul, to which he should grant all his things, whose happiness he should seek and strive for with every effort and exertion; whose torments he should flee and avoid at any cost. Indeed, it is better *to be in want for a short time, that you may possess for eternity, than to be charmed by a doubtful possession for a few days, only to be in need for all ages.* For just as we rightly call him the most foolish of mortals who buys a tiny, fleeting pleasure <note>original: *voluptatulam*. A diminutive of *voluptas*, emphasizing the insignificance and smallness of the joy.</note> at the price of eternal torments: so we shall recognize it as a mark of true wisdom to gain eternal things through the loss of temporal ones, or rather, *to use temporal things opportunely for the sake of eternity.*

### THE CHRISTIAN

Those who wish to extend the brevity of human life through their deeds, and desire to be inscribed in the temple of memory, torment themselves in various ways to obtain the immortality of a name. Hence, some commend the subtlety of their own genius to posterity through late-night studies <note>original: *lucubrationibus*. This refers to scholarly work or writing performed by lamplight during the night.</note>; others leave behind the industry and craftsmanship of their hands; still others insinuate themselves into history through sumptuous and magnificent buildings. Each of them, as they say, transmits the best part of themselves to a future age.

The Christian cares for none of these things with such anxiety. Instead, he always has **Farewell, world** <note>original: *Vale mundus*</note> upon his lips and in his heart. In departing from this life, he would even wish that his very memory could be snatched away from the world. "Ungrateful earth," he says, "you shall not even keep my name," and thus he exits the world entirely. 

But there is another way that remains for him to make himself immortal: almsgiving <note>original: *eleemosyna*. From the Greek word for mercy or pity; it refers here to charitable giving to the poor.</note>. Not that kind of charity, certainly, which requires trumpets, tablets, bells, rules, statutes, anniversaries, eulogies, and similar "ivies" <note>original: *hederas*. Likely a metaphor for the way vanity or public honors cling to a deed like ivy to a wall.</note> and attachments of a noisy generosity <note>original: *streperæ liberalitatis*. A "clattering" or "loud" style of giving intended for public display.</note>. Rather, he seeks the charity that is hidden even from the left hand of the giver <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 6:3: "But when you give to the needy, do not let your left hand know what your right hand is doing."</note>, known only to God. This is the kind of charity that takes the place of so many banquets and drinking parties <note>original: *symposiorum*. Traditional social gatherings centered on wine and conversation.</note>...

<summary>This page contrasts the worldly desire for fame—achieved through architecture, art, or scholarship—with the Christian ideal of humble anonymity. It argues that true immortality is found not in public monuments, but in secret acts of charity known only to God.</summary>

## XLII.

### *The Soul.*

The World <note>original: *Mundus*. In this context, "The World" refers to secular society and those who prioritize material life over spiritual concerns.</note> laughs when it sees a Christian so harsh toward his own body that he allows himself to taste none of this earth’s pleasures, and it looks down on him as if he were someone who does not know how to live. But this is absolutely necessary for whoever wishes to save his soul. For since the soul is joined to the body under this law—that it should have command over the body, and the body, in turn, should faithfully serve the soul—there is nothing the body finds more pleasing than to shake off the soul’s **yoke** and impose the harshest laws upon it. 

The end result of this is that while the body wallows and riots in pleasures for a time, the soul is simultaneously stained by this **filth** <note>original: *fimo*. Literally "manure" or "dung," used here as a strong metaphor for the degrading nature of purely physical indulgence.</note>, and in the end, both are sentenced to the punishments of **hell** <term>gehennæ: A term derived from the Valley of Hinnom, used in the New Testament to describe a place of final spiritual punishment or "hell."</term>. 

The opposite occurs when the soul restrains the body; for because the body easily forgets its own **foul origin** <note>A reference to the biblical idea that the human body was formed from the dust of the earth (Genesis 2:7).</note> and naturally inclines toward pride, it must never be allowed a more relaxed social life, or greater wealth, or more luxurious food without a **muzzle** <note>original: *fiſcella*. A small wicker basket or muzzle. The author suggests that the body's appetites must be physically "caged" or restricted like an animal.</note>, lest it be fatte—

He should accustom it to sweat and cold, withdraw it from the world's festivals, banquets, dances, and other lures of excess, and finally render it agile and ready to perform those things that please God and are profitable to the soul.

[The soul] disdains earthly things, and finding nothing worthy of itself, it must be fed and refreshed from heaven. Christ himself gave himself to this divine purpose, for he instituted an admirable food and drink in his most holy supper. Under the forms of bread and wine, he communicates and hands over his own Body—which suffered for us—and his own Blood—which was poured out for us. He does this not only to raise the soul into the hope of future blessedness, but also to comfort, recreate, animate, and gladden the soul with a <term>real presence: the theological doctrine that the actual body and blood of Christ are truly present in the Eucharist, rather than being merely symbolic</term> in this most sacred banquet. 

The wicked never eat this with impunity <note>original: *impune*. A reference to the biblical warning that those who partake in the Eucharist unworthily bring judgment upon themselves.</note>; however, the pious and faithful are filled with such infinite pleasure that they easily abhor all the feasts of the earth, recognizing that enough has been provided for their soul’s welfare, and enough delights have been prepared. 

Alas, however, for the darkness <note>original: *caliginem*. This refers to a spiritual or intellectual "fog" that prevents humans from seeing divine truth.</note> of the human mind! This very symbol of Christian concord <note>The author is lamenting how the Eucharist (the "Lord's Supper"), intended as a ritual of unity, became a central point of bitter disagreement during the Reformation.</note> has distracted brothers into various factions. Most of these trust their own ideas more than they trust Christ; they prefer to be scrupulous and argumentative about the specific words used, rather than simply making use of these most holy forms which are so full of mystery. Even those very people who boast that they hold faith in Christ most simply...

The Christian acts differently; he is able to be *alone*. Indeed, when he is in the midst of the greatest crowd, it is then that he is *most* alone. He is driven into himself by the crushing weight and the clashing of so many outcries and vanities, for it is impossible for him to approve of any of these things; instead, he stands apart from them all. For nothing truly associates or joins us together except holding the same opinion and the same sentiment; if these are foreign to you, then even in the middle of a whole army, you are alone and separate.

### XLIV.
### Retreat. <note>original: *Secessus*. This refers to a spiritual withdrawal or "stepping back" from the world for reflection.</note>

Behold, therefore, whether it be a remedy for marital chastity or for virginal purity: *retreat*, which the ancients valued most highly, and which a Christian cannot despise. This is a calling back of the soul to itself, or within itself, when—with external things set aside—you examine yourself; and so that you may do this more easily, you depart from the crowd and the noise. This can be done whenever there is leisure, but especially when Nature itself is silent. It can be done at length and with persistence, yet sometimes it is accomplished in a single moment. Indeed, just as prayers are sometimes prolonged, and not rarely "arrow-like" <term>jaculatoriæ: from the Latin *jaculum* (dart/javelin). In Christian tradition, "ejaculatory prayers" are short, intense mental prayers sent quickly to God throughout the day.</term>: so here, the movement of the mind toward God...

*Resolution*, I say, is what is missing—the firm will that seizes and carries out Divine promptings. Without this, no one has ever followed God, even if they have heard His call more than a thousand times. He was the most excellent man who, having read the life of Antony <note>Saint Antony of Egypt (c. 251–356), whose biography by Athanasius was a famous catalyst for spiritual conversion in the early Church.</note>, said: "If I wish, behold, I become a friend of God right now; I begin from this very hour, in this very place." And at once he turned his mind from the world, to which...

The world hates its own; let it also hate the Christian. It mocks its own; let it also mock the Christian. It despoils its own; let it also despoil the Christian. In whatever way it does not spare its own, let it not hold back from the Christian. If we cannot escape it in any way, let us stand on the side of those whose worldly losses are more than compensated by Divine blessings <term>beneficiis Divinis: spiritual gifts or graces bestowed by God.</term>—those for whom all this hatred, ridicule, and injury results in gain <term>lucrum: profit; used here to echo the biblical idea that "to die is gain" and that earthly suffering builds heavenly treasure.</term>.

## XLV.
### *The Infinite.*

It would go well with the human race if it did not begrudge itself the gifts granted from on high, nor hinder them, nor cast them away entirely. For God made man capable of cultivation <note>original: *culturæ*; in this context, the term refers to the intellectual and spiritual "tilling" of the soul to produce virtue and wisdom.</note>, and set before him an infinity of things in which he might exercise himself and find the highest pleasure.

Now, since a vast variety of things has been gathered together through the labors and industry of many—aided by the Supreme Power <term>Supremo numine: a common Latin phrase for God's divine providence or governing will.</term>—it is a wonder how foolishly we handle it: how some tear it apart, others throw it into confusion, others trample it, others defile it, others pass it by, others climb upon it <note>original: *inscendant*; this implies an arrogant attempt to master or dominate knowledge for selfish pride.</note>, others claim it for themselves, others dissect it, others corrupt it, and in every way treat it unjustly.

This is also the reason why this mass of human ingenuity <note>The author is likely referring to the "Information Age" of the 17th century—the explosion of books, scientific discoveries, and complex philosophies that he felt were becoming a burden rather than a benefit.</note> becomes less suited to a Christian, since it contributes less to public use, tranquility, and culture, but rather fills the World <term>Orbem: the world or the "globe" of human society.</term> with a disordered greatness, and hinders rather than adorns; things which, by right judgment and Christian rules, should be handled...

...otherwise it would be shaped, and without doubt it would cease to be Chaos, ending instead as a most beautiful and highly decorated temple of the earth, filled with every elegance. For forms <note>original: *species*; in this context, the specific types or categories of things in nature.</note> are not lacking, nor materials, nor the entire equipment for the structure; nor is there a lack of workers in great numbers. It would be a magnificent daring, O my Christ, and worthy of a Monarch, to gather the gifts of God dispersed throughout the world and the fruits of the more sublime geniuses under one command <term>Sub unum imperium: a metaphor for a unified system of thought or a single governing principle of knowledge.</term>, and to connect them to one another so that they might form one visible work—a summary of the world <note>original: *orbis breviarium*; a "breviary" or "abridgment" of the globe, essentially an encyclopedia or a unified system of all human understanding.</note>. 

But these things remain as mere wishes, such that they might seem like dreams rather than practical plans. And so the Christian walks about as a silent *spectator*, and from such a great diversity of works, such nobility of materials, and such skill in forms—and even from the genius of the blueprints—he takes immense pleasure, and gives thanks to God, the protector of the human race.

Amidst these choices, he hesitates, wondering whether he wishes to settle by *one* of the more excellent craftsmen and unfold every fold of his own industry <term>Industry (original: *industria*): in this period, the term refers to diligent skill, creative labor, or the focused application of the mind to a specific craft.</term>; or whether he should instead turn his eyes *through all* of them—not with a wandering gaze, but with an attentive and deliberate one. Yet, this latter path seems more fitting for a guest upon this earth, one who has desired in his soul to contemplate not just every detail of a single city, but the remarkable features of as many as possible. However, there is a need for a *golden chain* <note>original: *aurea cathena*; a common philosophical metaphor representing the interconnectedness of all reality, suggesting that all knowledge is linked in a single, divine sequence.</note> by which he may recognize the connections of human affairs and their combinations with one another. Without it, he might lose himself in a tumble of diverse forms; with it, he may pass from one to another in a proper order, returning from time to time to the primary sources of things and directing his contemplation back to that origin. 

For as soon as he loses sight of his center, or progresses so far that he cannot reach it with his right hand, he will wander lost in this *Labyrinth of the World* <note>original: *Mundi labyrintho*; a popular 17th-century metaphor for the confusing and deceptive nature of earthly life and fragmented knowledge when sought without a unifying divine principle.</note>, and he will never easily extricate himself unless led back by God Himself. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Citizen" (the Christian traveler) and his approach to observing the world. # THE CITIZEN

Amidst these things, he wonders whether he should sit beside *one* particular individual from among the more excellent craftsmen and unfold all the folds of his own industry <note>original: *industriæ suæ sinus evoluere*; a metaphor for dedicating one's full effort and capacity to a single pursuit.</note>, or whether he should cast his eyes—not wandering, but attentive and lingering—across them all. Truly, both paths provide grounds for the admiration of God, since each individual species acknowledges, praises, and reflects its Creator; nevertheless, the latter seems more fitting for a guest of this earth who has desired in his soul to contemplate not just the particulars of a single city, but the unique qualities of as many things as possible. 

However, there is need of a *golden chain* <term>Golden chain (original: *aurea cathena*): A philosophical concept, often tracing back to Homer and later adopted by Platonists and Christians, representing the hierarchical link between God and the physical world.</term>, by which he may recognize the connections of human affairs and their combinations among themselves. He must not wallow in diverse species <note>original: *speciebus*; here meaning types or varieties of things.</note> without purpose, but should instead pass from one to another in a decent order, and return repeatedly to the main sources of things, directing his contemplation back to that point. For as soon as he leaves his center <note>The "center" refers to God, the source of all order.</note> behind or progresses so far that he can no longer reach back to it with his right hand, he will wander lost in this labyrinth of the world, and will never easily disentangle himself unless he is led back by God himself. Therefore, it is even safer [to stay with] one...

As a result, while they find reasons to doubt everywhere, they find it difficult to finally reach a firm decision. 

But the Christian, who never values this universe more highly than God intended, acknowledges these temporal deceptions <term>Temporal deceptions (original: *temporis fallimenta*): The fleeting, often misleading attractions of the physical world that can distract from eternal spiritual truths.</term>, yet he does not let them enter his soul. They may please him, but they do not capture him; they may invite him, but they do not possess him.

THE CITIZEN

## XLVI.
### *Knowledge.*

Among those who have practiced philosophy with the greatest modesty, Socrates stands closer to Christians than any other. Even his famous axiom—in which he declared that he knew only one thing: that he knew nothing <note>original: *hoc axioma, quo se unum id scire, quod nihil sciat*</note>—deserves our praise. Believe me, this is not the confession of an ignorant man or someone feigning humility. Rather, it is the integrity <term>Integrity (original: *ingenuitas*): In this context, it refers to a combination of intellectual honesty and the noble character required to admit the limits of human understanding.</term> of a man who was granted the ability to struggle through all complexities, to penetrate everything, and to examine all things. Through this process, he recognized the darkness, the emptiness, and the uncertainty inherent in all worldly matters. 

Thus, he discovered the vanity of worshipping false gods, the absurdity of tyrannical rule, the foolish impulses of men, the shameful habits of the common crowd, and the frivolous chatter of other philosophers. Like an "unhappy physician," he walked among them, seeking to heal these ailments. 

The true Christian knows that he himself knows nothing, except for what the Holy Spirit <note>original: *spiritus sacer*</note> teaches, which is the most solid and certain knowledge of all. Furthermore, this Divine Teacher does not require a mind of great subtlety, but rather one that is pliable and receptive <note>original: *tractabili*</note>. Nor does He always require a long span of time to teach; if He sees fit, He can fill His disciple with wisdom in a single moment.

<meta>catchword: Suddenly (original: *Subito*)</meta>

<summary>This page compares the humility of Socrates to the Christian worldview, arguing that true knowledge begins with admitting one's ignorance and relying on the immediate, transformative teaching of the Holy Spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Christian philosophy, intellectual humility, Holy Spirit, divine wisdom, vanity of the world, Socratic ignorance</keywords>

If these people are forced to return by the lashes of Penance <note>original: *Pœnitentiæ flagellis*</note>, how they tremble! How they regret and feel shame for their faults! Indeed, they never truly escape the welts of those lashes, and they are without the gore <note>The original Latin uses *carent* (to lack or be without). The author suggests that while the "welts" or scars of sin remain forever, the flowing "gore"—perhaps the active, weeping corruption of the wound—is what they might eventually be free from, though the marks of the punishment endure.</note> that flows from them. So thick is the smoke of our ruined nature that even when we acknowledge we know nothing, we also wish to learn nothing. We are so malicious that we seize upon, refine, and pass on every wicked thing, showing the greatest possible aptitude for learning; but we listen to, receive, possess, and handle good things slowly, coldly, stupidly, and sleepily. We show ourselves to be born for evil and driven toward the good only by necessity.

They act rightly who draw Christian Truth <note>original: *Veritatem Christianam*</note>—and especially from the fountain of truth, Christ—and [the] streams how-

## XLVIII.

### *Reason*

Although the earthly globe <note>original: *Globus terrenus*. The author refers to the physical world and the human societies inhabiting it.</note> obeys human reason, it is nevertheless governed in a most irrational manner. While man takes great pleasure in his own reason—and indeed, not infrequently sets himself up against God—it can hardly be expressed how much he confuses all things when he lacks [true] reason. For if anyone were to look more closely at the web of empires, the endeavors of men, their movements, vows, labors, honors, and indeed all the glory of the world, he would find no reason at all; instead, everything is driven either by force, or by fraud, or by agreement, or...

by chance <note>original: *casu*. This word likely follows "by whim" from the previous page.</note>, he will clearly perceive that all things consist of chance. Those very things which have a reputation—or more truly, a noise—of wisdom and prudence, if they are examined according to true reason, do not bear the light, but rather seek out darkness as soon as possible; nay, indeed, our reason hardly ever possesses reason, so that you should expect nothing solid here at all. The Christian unceasingly repeats this within himself: *Why do I do this, why do I say this, why do I want this, why do I avoid this, why do I refuse this? Why does the World do this?* It resists God, believes in Satan, and does such things which, by the mere question alone, put Vanity to shame and convict it of error.

For why should the wealthy man be money-grubbing? Why should the learned man be a mere man of letters <note>original: *literator*. And yet, these are our daily realities. The World consists of and is filled with these absurdities. ### XLIX.

#### *Immutable.*

It is in the interest of the Christian republic not only to be instructed with the best words, but also...

<summary>This page concludes a meditation on the power of speech, contrasting the harmful worldly tongue with the "Christian tongue" which heals and praises God. It then begins a new chapter on the "Immutable" or unchanging nature of the Christian community.</summary>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the section titled "Immutable" (Section XLIX), transitioning from the importance of instruction to the power of lived examples of steadfastness.</meta>

<page-num>177</page-num>
<header>THE CHRISTIAN.</header>

...also to be strengthened by praiseworthy examples.

Such a thing is the Christian: a noble specimen of immobility, who is moved from his position by no violence and no persuasion. *Indeed, anyone who seriously puts on God is immutable; for he neither sees nor finds anything more desirable than what he has already learned from God.* <note>original: *quisquis ferio Deum induit*. This phrasing echoes the biblical exhortation to "put on the Lord Jesus Christ" (Romans 13:14), suggesting that the believer’s character is entirely enveloped by the divine.</note> In this he stands firm, upon this foundation he leans; whatever the world may do or say—whether it threatens or flatters, compels or persuades, commands or begs—it gains nothing from such a Christian. Let no one believe such people could exist if they had not already lived; and the World itself testifies with indignation: for as often as it recounts the various kinds of death inflicted upon Christians, it reproaches itself with its own unshakeable victors. This, finally, is the Christian boundary: to yield to no evil, and to depart from life rather than from the agreement with what is true and good. But few—alas, how few—of this kind might you find today, who like Elijah <note>A reference to the Hebrew prophet who stood alone against the royal authority of King Ahab and the prophets of Baal.</note> would oppose themselves to an entire kingdom; or who, like Daniel’s companions <note>Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego, who refused to worship a golden idol and remained standing while the rest of the kingdom prostrated themselves; they were subsequently thrown into a "clibanum" (furnace or oven).</note>, would stand upright while everyone else is bowed down, and await the furnace. For nothing today is more frequent than accommodation—

<sig>H 5</sig>

<summary>This page defines the Christian ideal of "immutability," or unshakeable constancy in faith, which remains unmoved by worldly threats or bribes. It cites the historical steadfastness of martyrs and biblical figures like Elijah as models for resisting social and political pressure.</summary>

...nothing is more common than pretense and concealment. Wherever the *north wind* <note>original: *aquilo*; used here as a metaphor for the cold winds of adversity or shifting political power.</note> blows, everyone bows down, even those whom we believed to be the columns of faith; indeed, even those who, in peaceful times, boasted of themselves with great arrogance. The magistrate yields, the senator yields, the prelate yields; what solid strength can you expect from the common people? And yet, in truth, you may sooner find among the obscure those who bend no knee to *Baal* <note>A reference to the Canaanite deity; here symbolizing the act of compromising one's faith to please a worldly power.</note> than among the eminent and famous.

O how steadfast are the former, O how fickle are the latter! O how useful are the former, O how ruinous are the latter, for whom a *millstone* <note>original: *lapis molaris*; an allusion to the biblical warning that it is better to have a millstone hung around one's neck than to lead others into sin.</note> hung around the neck would be more fitting than a golden chain or a symbol of worldly favor. Grant, O God, that I may love those who are unshakeable, however humble; grant that I be not offended by the changeable, however exalted; grant, O God, that I may *stand, desire, strive, excel, and persevere* for You.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What should a Christian do if the injustice of tyranny should overturn the *laws of the Fatherland*, and most especially *freedom of religion*, contrary to faith and law, and should decree exile for the innocent? Should he yield if the matter concerns the public safety to which he is bound, and allow [the tyrant] to act willfully against those who lack counsel and wisdom? Who... <note>original: *Ecquis*; the page ends mid-word, leading into the catchword *quis*.</note>

What God established this law: that what one man thinks, everyone must believe, or else leave their native soil? That those whom one person wants killed, all should offer their throats? That what God has distributed to everyone should depend on the whim of a single person? None of these things ever entered the mind of God—neither to permit such power to one man, nor to impose such a burden upon all. It is a sign of both human malice and stupidity to demand such things under the fear of religion. 

God committed to those who rule the care, safety, preservation, and protection of those entrusted to them; He did not make the people their subjects <note>The author distinguishes between "care" (stewardship) and total "subjection," where the ruler owns the people.</note> or hand over their blood and wealth; He did not even wish for the most free mind to be enslaved. This nobility of spirit once stirred the *Maccabees* <note>original: *Machabæos*; leaders of a Jewish rebel army who fought against the Seleucid Empire for religious freedom.</note> to oppose the monstrosity of *Antiochus* <note>Antiochus IV Epiphanes, a Greek king who attempted to suppress Jewish customs.</note>; this freed the *Swiss* <note>original: *Helvetios*; likely referring to the Swiss struggle for independence from the Holy Roman Empire.</note> from a harsh yoke; and this, in a very recent example, restored the *Belgians* <note>original: *Belgas*; a reference to the Dutch Revolt or Eighty Years' War (1568–1648), where the Low Countries fought for independence from Spanish rule.</note> to wholeness. 

But here one must diligently guard against the <term>mixture of the flesh</term> <note>original: *carnis mixtura*; a theological term referring to worldly, selfish, or prideful motives as opposed to purely spiritual ones.</note>, lest while we pretend to serve the cause of Religion or liberty, we are found to be acting for our own interests, which has been the ruin of many. God desires pure and simple ends, and requires for them the most whole and right mea—

Meanwhile, the Christian believes timidly but acts bravely; he approaches a task with reluctance but pursues it persistently; he opposes others unwillingly, but if he sees that God entirely wills it so, he resists courageously and firmly.

### 50.

#### *Poverty.*

So that the Christian may show himself to be completely contrary to the World, he chooses poverty above all treasures. Not indeed that poverty which lacks or is in need of all physical things, but that which humbles the man himself in the sight of *God*. This can exist even under a crown and scepters, and among vast treasures. For it is only ***poverty of Spirit, or annihilation*** <note>original: *annihilatio*; in 17th-century mystical theology, this refers to the total "emptying" of the self or the soul's surrender of its own will to God.</note>, where the Christian [depends] entirely on God’s...

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on Godly resistance to tyranny, emphasizing that it must be done with humility and without the spirit of rebellion. It then introduces the concept of "poverty of spirit," arguing it is an internal state of self-surrender that can be maintained even by the wealthy or powerful.</summary>

<keywords>Christian ethics, rebellion, divine providence, spiritual poverty, humility, annihilation of self, political resistance, virtue</keywords>

<page-num>181</page-num>

...mercy depends. He recognizes nothing as his own, indeed he recognizes himself as nothing. Our Germans once called this ***Gelassenheit*** <note>original: *Die Gelaffenheit*; a central concept in German mysticism, particularly in the works of Meister Eckhart and Jakob Böhme, referring to a state of "yieldedness," detachment, or the total surrender of the individual will to God.</note>, by which a person gives themselves entirely to God, so that He may use them as if with His own hand, moved by no recognition, estimation, passion, or affection of one’s own. 

To be sure, just as our limbs do not know what they do, but obey the command of the soul—they see, hear, and touch, and they do so neither reluctantly nor boastfully <note>original: *gloriosula*; literally "with a little vainglory."</note>. Thus, whoever obtains this from himself—so that neither out of love for the Heavens nor out of fear of **Hell** <note>original: *gehennæ*</note> does he freely comply with God as his creator, recognizing or feeling nothing of his own will or love—he has truly left himself behind and transcended the symptoms <note>original: *symptomata*; in this context, the outward signs or conditions of being bound to physical, "fleshy" desires.</note> of human flesh. 

This, however, is an idea of **perfection** <note>original: *perfectionis*</note> rather than something that closely fits any mortal; yet it serves the Christian so that he may more easily recognize his own imperfections and strive toward more perfect things with every effort and endeavor. For this **poverty** <note>original: *pauperies*</note> is the highest **wealth** <note>original: *opulentia*</note>, by which we withdraw and elevate ourselves from all things that are below the nobility of the soul, so that the most noble...

...and that we may love and possess something [supreme]. This indeed is God, who is as much worthier of man's highest love as all creatures are unworthier. For from the **One** all things come, and to the **One** all things must return; and nothing is as hateful to God as the **duality** <term>binary</term> <note>original: *binario*; in mystical thought, this represents the soul divided between God and the world, rather than being unified in God.</note> or the halved. He demands the whole and entire man, beholden to no one either in heaven or outside of heaven; which German devotion has elegantly expressed in the person of God: *Either be entirely mine, or let it be not at all.* <note>original German: *Entweder gar mein; oder laß gar sein.* This proverb emphasizes that God accepts no half-measures in devotion.</note>

Nothing today is less in need of **beggars** <note>original: *mendicantibus*; this likely refers both to common beggars and to the mendicant religious orders, whom the author views critically here.</note>, yet nothing lacks them more. Why do we not act instead so that no one could or would dare to do that which might overturn their family’s estate, while ensuring the needy lack nothing necessary?

### ->God<-

## LII.
### *The Church.*

It remains for the Christian to include himself within the Church. Although she is scattered throughout the world and bound together by the invisible bonds of the Holy Spirit, she nevertheless dwells in certain lodgings <note>original: "hospitia"</note> here and there, and manifests herself through the purity of the Divine Word and the generous distribution of the Sacraments <term>Sacraments: sacred rites considered to be outward signs of inward grace, such as Baptism and the Eucharist</term>. Within this community, the Christian believes, prays, labors, suffers, and performs the duties of the holy family; for her sake, he pours out his wealth, his blood, and his very body. He listens to her, observes her, protects her, and accompanies her even unto the heavens. 

O most majestic *choir*, in which the Fathers, Patriarchs, Prophets, Apostles, Doctors <note>learned teachers and scholars of theology</note>, and Confessors <note>those who maintained their faith under trial or persecution without being martyred</note> follow Christ in a proper and unbroken succession down to our own day, wherever He may go. They resound with "Hallelujah" and attribute salvation, glory, honor, and power to the Lord our God <note>A reference to the song of the multitude in Revelation 19:1</note>. O golden City of God, like unto pure glass, whose walls are made of jasper and whose gates are made of twelve pearls <note>A description of the New Jerusalem from Revelation 21</note>...

<summary>This page concludes a list of Christian virtues and begins a poetic description of the Church as both a global spiritual body and a historical community of saints and martyrs.</summary>
<keywords>Church, Holy Spirit, Sacraments, Christian duties, Succession of Saints, City of God, Hallelujah</keywords>

# TO TOBIAS HESS
## TO A MAN
### OF UPRIGHT HEART, HIS MOST BELOVED AND GREATLY LONGED-FOR FRIEND.

<image-desc>A decorative historiated initial 'H' featuring floral and leaf patterns.</image-desc>

The "Crossroads of Hercules" <note>original: "Herculis bivium"; a classical allegory where the hero Hercules must choose between the difficult path of Virtue and the easy path of Pleasure</note> is famous in literature, and every one of the grammarians or dictionary-makers claims its right-hand path for themselves. Whoever departs from them by even a hair's breadth <note>original: "latum unguem"; literally "a fingernail's width"</note> is already said to be wandering astray on that other road, the path of Pleasure. Meanwhile, the way of Christ remains entirely hidden; and when it is presented as something cheap and lowly, it is treated as a joke. Evidently, we must first play the fool, cause an uproar, and run to and fro; and afterwards—

Although it has happened by our own lamentable misfortune <note>original: "malo nostro lamentabili"; likely a reference to the Fall of Man and the subsequent loss of dominion over nature</note> that the sky—so beautiful and visible, a vast portion of our Empire <note>original: "Imperij nostri"; referring here to the physical realm or the natural world inhabited by humanity</note>—has been imposed upon us as both a ruler and a torturer, and afflicts our bodies with infinite diseases just as it agitates our minds with various errors and obscurities: Nevertheless, after Christ—by his glorious and likewise healing rising <note>original: "exortu"; the author uses an astronomical term for "rising" to contrast Christ with the stars</note>—not only did not subject himself to the stars at all, but he completely delivered all those who give their names to his NAME from their Empire and tyranny. Truly, those among them who either fear the heavens or stretch out conquered hands <note>original: "victas porrigunt manus"; a classical idiom for surrendering or admitting defeat</note> in submission to them, are injurious toward their Christ and Christian liberty. Furthermore, those who actually consult the stars, and seek from them judgment and prophecy regarding themselves, are plainly blasphemous and intolerable.

**STIANUS COSMOXENUS** <term>Cosmoxenus</term> <note>original: "Cosmoxenus"; a hybrid term from the Greek *kosmos* (world) and *xenos* (stranger), referring to a Christian who lives in the world but is not of it.</note>, *he who is indeed the offspring of human weakness and misery, but a new and regenerated creature of commiserating and prevenient* **Grace** <note>original: "Gratiæ"; "prevenient grace" is a theological term for the divine influence that precedes human decision and inclines the soul toward God.</note>, *completely patient with the present, and eager for the future.* **To the Earth** *he is not only a guest, but dead to it, and in no part of himself suited to it; he is like a monster* <note>original: "monstrum"; in this period, a "monster" or "prodigy" referred to something that defied the natural order, often used here to describe how the spiritual man appears freakish or incomprehensible to worldly people.</note> *among scoffers, but held in* **God's** *own delights. He is altogether such a one whose life is lived not here, but elsewhere; who rejoices in his joy not outwardly, but inwardly; whose knowledge is known not on the outside, but within; by whose wish nothing in the* **WORLD** *that is great, celebrated, sublime, noisy, or luxurious is sought. Rather, he is satisfied only by those things which satisfy no one else: namely, things that are absent, hoped for, and believed in—and which, as the maliciously wicked laugh, are "imaginary." But let us examine him through the* **Astrological** *headings.*

He proposes to analyze this spiritual life using the traditional categories and headings of astrology.</summary>

### CHAPTER I.

->*Concerning the Lord of the Geniture.*<- <note>original: "De Domino Genituræ"; in astrology, the "Lord of the Geniture" is the planet that has the most influence over a person's birth chart. Here, the author uses it as a metaphor for the ruling power within a person's soul.</note>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut drop cap 'D' decorated with scrolling vines and floral patterns.</image-desc>

CONCERNING the lordship of the Geniture, or of the house, two most different figures struggle amongst themselves: **ADAM** and **CHRIST**; the one corporeal, the other 
<margin>1.</margin> 
spiritual; the former lazy, sleepy, heavy, and inept; the latter brisk, vigilant, sublime, and wise. Each seeks the entire dwelling for himself, nor can he tolerate the other. But **CHRIST** conquers, through the prayers, sighs, and trials of the *Reborn* <note>original: "Renati"; referring to the "regenerate" Christian who has been spiritually awakened.</note>, yet not in such a way that no snares from the opposing side come to be cast down by him from time to time. Instead, he sustains the daily assaults of **ADAM** in such a way that he never yields the possession of so beloved, select, and sacred a house—or **Temple** <note>original: "Templi"; a reference to the body as the temple of the Holy Spirit.</note>—to him; rather, having blocked up the windows, cracks, and openings of the senses and appetites, [he defends] his bulwark...

<summary>The author describes the internal struggle for the "lordship" of a person's life as a battle between the fallen nature (Adam) and the spiritual nature (Christ). Christ is depicted as the victor in the "Reborn" person, though the struggle against the senses and the "Old Adam" is a daily defense.</summary>

<keywords>Lord of the Geniture, Adam, Christ, Spiritual Warfare, Rebirth, Temple of the Soul, Senses, Astrology</keywords>

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page concludes Chapter I’s astrological metaphors and begins Chapter II, which applies the medical theory of the "four humors" to the spiritual life of the believer.</meta>

renders it more fortified and firm, and more safely opposes it to Satan and the World, two other most furious enemies. Therefore, that salvific *Star of Jacob* <note>original: "Stella Iacob"; a biblical reference to Numbers 24:17, traditionally interpreted as a prophecy of the Messiah.</note> predominates; the *Sun of Justice* <note>original: "Iusticiæ Sol"; a reference to Malachi 4:2.</note> predominates; the *Light from on high* <note>original: "Lux ab alto"; a reference to Luke 1:78.</note> predominates. Even if the remaining stars are ill-omened and can neither predict nor grant anything good, they are nevertheless weaker and are overcome by a thousand thousand testimonies.

---

### CHAPTER II.
#### *Concerning Temperament.* <note>In historical medicine and alchemy, "temperament" refers to the balance of the four bodily fluids or "humors" thought to determine a person's physical health and character.</note>

There are four humors belonging to the Adamic offspring <note>original: "Adamaticæ prolis"; referring to humanity in its fallen state inherited from Adam.</note>: the **Hot**, which makes people angry, proud, and tumultuous; the **Moist**, which makes them dissolute, wavering, and slaves to the belly <note>original: "ventris mancipia"</note> and to Pleasure; the **Cold**, which makes them godless, passive, and stupid; and the **Dry**, which makes them greedy, fruitless, and unmerciful. None of these qualities suit Christ, nor our Christian man; nor does any mixture of these humors create a temperament worthy of the *Reborn* <note>original: "Renato"; the spiritually awakened or "regenerate" soul.</note>. Therefore, other humors must dwell within him; and for us, **Heat** shall be **Charity**...

<summary>The author concludes that Christ's spiritual influence overcomes negative astrological omens and begins a new chapter reimagining the four traditional humors as spiritual virtues for the "reborn" Christian.</summary>
<keywords>Star of Jacob, Sun of Justice, Astrology, Four Humors, Adamic nature, Reborn soul, Charity, Temperament</keywords>

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual "humors" from the previous page. Having defined Heat as Charity, the author now defines Coldness, Moisture, and Dryness in the context of the "Reborn" soul, concluding Chapter II and beginning Chapter III on character.</meta>

devotion, the digestion of evils <note>original: "concoctio"; in early modern medicine, this referred to the process of ripening or "cooking" humors to restore health, here applied to the processing of one's sins.</note>, and the burning away of vanity. *Coldness* is forbearance, indulgence, the forgetting of evil, and a sacred simplicity. *Moisture* is the watering of the Word of God, the streams of generosity, the soul melted in God, and the voyage to the harbor. *Dryness* is the endurance of the Cross, insensibility toward the world, solidity, constancy, and truth. From these, a temperament shall be formed into the *Image of God*, which completely renounces its own will, love, and honor, and resigns itself into the hands of God; it reflects its God as if in a mirror, breathes Him, tastes Him <note>original: "sapit"; which carries the dual meaning of "tasting" and "being wise."</note>, looks toward Him, labors for Him, strives for Him, and finds rest in Him.

## CHAPTER III.
### *Concerning character and disposition.* <note>original: "De moribus, & ingenio." In this period, "ingenium" referred to one's natural temperament or innate talent.</note>

From this, a judgment concerning character is easily made, for they will be composed, humble, simple, and upright, but greatly shrinking from the customs of men. For this reason, such a person will easily marvel at nothing, except for his Christ, the supreme Miracle. And although he loves all men, he loves few, and in them <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword for the next page is "pauca" (few).</note>

<summary>The author redefines the four classical humors as spiritual virtues that coalesce to form the "Image of God" within a believer. This transformation results in a humble, Christ-centered character that remains detached from worldly society.</summary>
