He will not seek revenge for insults <note>original: "infamiam"; refers to the loss of reputation or public disgrace.</note>. He will not push himself into public office. He will not tremble at death. He will, however, be open to everyone and ready to help; he will expect nothing from others except love. He will belong to others; those things that belong to others will not be his, nor will he crave them. He will endure rather than strike <note>original: "feret non feriet"; a wordplay highlighting the shift from aggression to patient endurance.</note>. And amidst all this, he will be very silent <note>original: "filebit," a common printer's error for "silebit" (he will be silent).</note>, he will be very much alone with himself, and he will sigh often. In his natural disposition, he will be teachable in the school of Christ, dovelike in his conduct, and snakelike <note>A reference to the biblical command in Matthew 10:16 to be "wise as serpents and innocent as doves."</note> in his watchfulness against enemies. He will be completely deaf and senseless to the voices of the World, but above all, he will be most diligent, most clear-sighted, and most hardworking in scrubbing away and casting out the filth contracted from

# C A P. I V.

### *On the Stature, Form, and Appearance*
### *of the Body.*

There is indeed a certain excellence common to all human beings: that they stand upright, unlike the posture of all other animals. Furthermore, the human being represents the structure of this entire Universe as if in a summary <note>original: "compendio"; this refers to the popular Renaissance idea of man as a "microcosm" or a miniature version of the entire universe.</note>, and from the "spoils" of almost all other creatures, humans are able to abundantly satisfy the persistent demands of Nature. 

But for our Christian, there is something truly and uniquely most glorious: that his Christ assumed this same bodily form, shape, stature, and even its physical needs <note>original: "indigentiam"; referring to the human frailties like hunger, thirst, and fatigue that Jesus experienced.</note>. He walked with this body, was raised up to the highest heavens, and was received at the right hand of God. Indeed, the Christian marvels at his own body with trembling and adoration—not only because he hears God speaking with its ears, gazes upon the Great Works <note>original: "Magnalia"; a term used in the Bible (e.g., Acts 2:11) to describe the mighty and miraculous deeds of God.</note> of God with its eyes, and talks with God and acts as His interpreter with its tongue, but most of all because the body is a lodging-place and a sanctuary for the Divine Body. Afterwards, he governs it in such a way,

<meta>catchword: governs,</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter IV, discussing the dignity of the human body. It argues that while humans are naturally superior to animals due to their upright posture and role as a microcosm of the universe, the body’s true glory for a Christian lies in the fact that Christ shared in human form and elevated it to heaven.</summary>

<keywords>human dignity, upright posture, microcosm, Incarnation of Christ, body as sanctuary, Magnalia Dei, Christian anthropology</keywords>

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page concludes the previous discussion on the governance of the body (Chapter IV) and transitions into a new chapter concerning spiritual health and the character of the Christian. It continues the theme of the body as a vessel for the divine, prioritizing spiritual salvation over physical perfection.</meta>

...governs it, so that he might bring one who is one-eyed, maimed, or lame <note>This is a clear allusion to the Gospel of Matthew 18:8-9, where Jesus suggests it is better to enter eternal life physically impaired than to be cast into fire with a whole body.</note> to the promised heavenly joys, rather than excluding one who is whole—through ruinous soft living <note>original: "pernitiosa mollitie"; refers to a life of luxury, ease, or lack of discipline that weakens the spirit.</note>—from such an ineffable glorification of human flesh.

### CHAPTER V.

->*On Habits and*<-
->*Health.*<-

There are no diseases here, as long as Christ—that perfect and ever-blessed Physician—is present. Indeed, to transgress His diet and prescribed care does not merely expose one to disease, but is itself the most dangerous disease. The world’s air, the world’s company, and the world’s contagion are most pestilential; they leave no one untouched—or rather, uninfected—whoever departs from his Christ. Let anyone who wishes to be safe from this plague, leprosy, and fever keep himself at home and dwell with his own little soul <note>original: "animula"; a diminutive and affectionate term for the soul, suggesting its delicacy and the need for internal reflection.</note>, nor should anyone hope for medicine anywhere if they neglect Christ’s antidote. Christ is the only health; outside of Christ, there are nothing but diseases,

<vocab>Christ, Physician, Disease, Leprosy, Fever, Health, Glory</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes Chapter IV by arguing that physical infirmity is preferable to spiritual ruin, then opens Chapter V by presenting Christ as the sole Physician capable of curing the "pestilence" of the world. It emphasizes that staying close to Christ and internal reflection are the only defenses against spiritual contagion.</summary>
<keywords>Christ as Physician, spiritual health, worldly contagion, discipline of the flesh, salvation, leprosy, soul</keywords>

...who toss and turn men in their beds, wearing them down and flaying the very flesh from their bones <note>original: "excarnificant"; literally to strip the flesh off, implying intense physical or mental agony.</note>. Therefore, our **Reborn Man** <note>original: "Renatus"; likely referring to the "reborn" or regenerate Christian as a personified archetype.</note> is always strong, always well; the diseases that scourge the flesh and merely pull the skin tight do not truly touch him. Instead, like a healing ***Myrrh***, they preserve him from decay <note>Myrrh was used in antiquity both as a healing balm and an embalming agent to prevent rot; the author suggests that physical suffering acts as a preservative for the soul.</note>, crowning him with a hero's **Laurel** as an exemplar of true constancy, enduring patience, and a hope and trust that has been proven through trial.

### CHAPTER VI.

->*On other accidents of the body, such as*<-
->*falling, ruin, and imprisonment.*<-

The path upon which our ***Reborn Man*** shall walk is both narrow and rugged, overgrown with thorns and rocks, where there is constant danger of falling and of bruising one's feet. But there is One who may lift him up: Christ, who so seasons those first painful blows that they truly become sweet. Thus, there is nothing good he should expect from the World, except that he finally develops a thick skin <note>original: "callum"; a callus. The author implies that the only "gift" the world gives is the hardening of the spirit through repeated hardship.</note> and grows hardened against evils. Truly, in Christ he will find all things safe, level, pleasant, and secure, under whose protect— <meta>catchword: protection (tutela)</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on spiritual health by framing suffering as a preservative like myrrh, before opening a new chapter on how Christ protects the believer through the "accidents" and physical dangers of a difficult life.</summary>

<keywords>Reborn Man, Christ as Protector, Myrrh, Constancy, Spiritual Trials, The Narrow Path, Endurance</keywords>

--- Page 229 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter VI regarding the "Reborn Man's" safety under divine protection, specifically addressing imprisonment and the release of the soul. It then transitions into Chapter VII, which explores the spiritual genealogy of the believer.</meta>

...under whose protection he will never stumble by chance; it will always work for his benefit, and even if he is cast down, he will not be abandoned. Even the threats of prison—which the **Flesh**, the **World**, and **Satan** (those ill-fated stars) <note>original: "infaustæ stellæ"; the author uses astrological imagery to describe the three traditional enemies of the Christian soul as "bad omens" or "unlucky stars" that predict hardship.</note> portend—and the chains that lie in ambush in every corner, will do no harm. Nor will they hold him bound for long; but just as a heavy and burdensome body finally releases the free and ethereal ***Soul***, so shall his enemies (even against their will) see the Christian—snatched away by Christ the Liberator—restored to his former and even greater liberty.

### CHAPTER VII.

#### *On Parents, Blood-relations, and Paternal Kin.* <note>original: "agnatis"; in Roman law, "agnati" were relatives through the male line, whereas "consanguinei" refers to blood relations generally.</note>

Indeed, it must be admitted that our "newborn" <note>The "Reborn Man" introduced in previous chapters.</note> is descended from the lowliest parents, the most abject and even outlawed: his father, the sinner ***Adam***, and his mother, the cursed ***Earth***. From them, he inherits no wealth and can hope for no **Dignity**. But the ***Star of Jacob*** <note>A reference to Numbers 24:17, traditionally interpreted in Christian theology as a prophecy of the birth of Christ.</note> foretells for him a royal adoption, and a Father...

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on physical trials by arguing that even imprisonment and death serve to liberate the soul into Christ's freedom. It then begins a new chapter on the spiritual lineage of the "Reborn Man," contrasting his lowly earthly origin (Adam and Earth) with his divine adoption.</summary>

<keywords>protection, imprisonment, Soul and Body, Christ the Liberator, Chapter VII, genealogy of the soul, Adam, Star of Jacob, royal adoption</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter VII regarding the spiritual genealogy of the "Reborn Man." The previous page ended mid-sentence, introducing God as the new Father of the believer.</meta>

...and gives him *God* as a father, and the *Church* as a mother. From this comes not only the soul's kinship with God through its heavenly origin, but also the body's blood-relationship with *Christ*. <note>In 17th-century theology, "consanguinitas" (blood-relationship) often refers to the union between Christ and the believer's physical body through the Incarnation and the Eucharist.</note> Grafted into this family, he entirely renounces his shameful origin; he diligently takes care lest he stain the glory of his adoption with any remnants of his first birth, or bring them before the face of God his Father, who abhors them. Indeed, he never appears in the sight of the heavenly Father unless covered and wrapped in the garment of his *Christ*. <note>This refers to the "Robe of Righteousness," a common metaphor for the belief that God views the believer through the perfection of Christ rather than their own sins.</note> So great is the unpleasant presence, the nausea, and the hatred of the filthy *Adam* <note>The "Old Adam" represents human nature in its fallen, sinful state.</note> in the heavenly Palace and the kingdom of *Christ*. And this pride <note>original: "fastus"; the author is using a paradox here, suggesting that "priding oneself" on a new spiritual identity is actually a form of deep humility before God.</note> is still the lowest humility; would that we all were so proud, and most loftily renounced such an unlucky inheritance from *Adam*.

## CHAPTER VIII.

### *On Brothers, sisters, in-laws, etc.*

From these same different Fathers, there arises also a double line of brothers and sisters: one Jacobitic, the other Esauitic... <note>The author uses "Jacobitic" and "Esauitic" to categorize humanity into two spiritual lineages, based on the biblical twins Jacob (the chosen) and Esau (the rejected).</note>

<summary>This page describes the believer’s transition from a "shameful" earthly ancestry to a divine family, where God is Father and the Church is Mother. It emphasizes the need to renounce the "Old Adam" and appear before God only when "clothed" in Christ's righteousness.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual adoption, Church as Mother, blood-relationship with Christ, Original Sin, Jacob and Esau, garment of righteousness</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter VIII regarding the two lineages of humanity: the "Jacobitic" (spiritual/elect) and the "Esauitic" (worldly/rejected). It picks up from the previous page's introduction of these two groups.</meta>

...lineage: in great numbers on both sides, but the number of Jacobites <note>The author uses the biblical figures of Jacob and Esau to represent two types of people: the "Jacobites" are those reborn in spirit, while the "Esauitic" are those who remain in their fallen, worldly nature.</note> is always far smaller. Among the former, there is love, harmony, instruction, mutual aid, counsel, shared suffering, gentleness, and equality in Christ. Among the latter, there are pretenses, madness, slaughter, plunder, hatred, slanders, discords, pride, titles, and a shameful and most ambitious distinction between one another. 

In the first group, a brother cherishes his brother as a heavenly being; he looks up to him as a father or teacher, loves him as a son, and supports and corrects him as a fellow human. In the second group, a brother worships his brother as an Idol, or else one brother tramples, slaughters, and tortures another like a beast; brother feasts on the flesh of brother, and drinks his sweat and blood. <note>This graphic imagery of cannibalism and drinking "sweat and blood" is a rhetorical device used to describe the harsh reality of social and economic exploitation in the worldly "Esauitic" society.</note>

Therefore, our reborn man recognizes only the Jacobites as brothers; he pities the others, but avoids them and guards himself against them as much as possible. He sacredly, purely, and steadfastly cherishes *that Brotherhood which Jesus, the first and supreme brother, established,* and together with the remaining members, he forms

->**One, harmonious,**<-
->**and divine Body.**<-

<summary>This page contrasts the virtuous, egalitarian community of spiritual "Jacobites" with the violent, exploitative, and status-obsessed world of the "Esauitic" lineage. It defines true brotherhood as a divine unity established by Christ.</summary>

## CHAPTER IX.
### Concerning Friends, Companions, and Associates.

He will have no friend except Him who is everything to him: *Christ*. All others, no matter what their affection may be, never ascend to the perfection of true love. It is Christ who loves, teaches, corrects, and sustains the reborn person; and finally, at God’s judgment seat, He excuses, defends, and frees through His fruitful intercession the one who is destitute of all personal or outside help. Yet this same Christ demands a reciprocation of true love—a love that transcends the possession of parents, children, wives, and friends, and above all, transcends love of self. It is a love that must be able to endure the test of fire, water, the noose, the sword <note>original: *ferri*, literally "iron," but used here to denote a blade or weapon.</note>, torments, and all manner of trials. 

He will gladly join companions to himself, but they must be companions of the sacred race <note>original: *curriculi sacri*. The author uses the metaphor of a race or a track to describe the progression of the spiritual life.</note>, with whom he will strive with devotion and zeal, and upon whom he will reflect the rays of grace. These companions will share in his prayers, meditations, and sacred labors; they are his associates and fellow students in the School 

<meta>catchword: of the Lord (Dominicae)</meta>

of the Lord <note>original: *Dominicæ*, referring back to the "School of the Lord" mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note>, companions in endurance, steadfastness, and contempt for the world; not companions of drinking bouts, conflicts, idle chatter <note>original: *discursationum*, which implies wandering talk or social flitting about.</note>, games, and vanity. Indeed, familiarity with others will be extremely sparse, as it carries much filth with it and greatly disturbs that internal intimacy with Christ and the soul—and even with natural reason itself.

## CHAPTER X.

### *Concerning Enemies and Adversaries.*

There are enough enemies everywhere; one will not be safe even if he serves the World most devotedly. The World ridicules, mocks, and harasses with enmities even those whom it uses most, those whom it finds ready to serve it, and those whom it promotes. Therefore, that hatred alone is glorious which is diverted onto the Christian because of a hatred for Christ. By this, the Reborn person <note>The author uses *Renatus* (the Reborn) to describe the person who has undergone a spiritual transformation.</note> is not at all moved, disturbed, or provoked to snarl <note>original: *ringitur*, a vivid verb meaning to show one's teeth or growl like a dog; the author suggests the spiritual person does not snap back at critics.</note>, because he is generously compensated by the love of God. Thus, let our Reborn person be infested with laughter, calumnies, lies, rebukes, threats, and imprecations—

with imprecations, blows, obstacles, torments, and temptations, nothing casts him down: but toward the truth once revealed and the good once shown, he pierces through a thousand envious tongues, a thousand malicious hands, and a thousand satirical and sarcastic pens. He is certain that no weapon is hard enough to split the Christian-Rock <note>original: *Christianisaxum*—a compound word likely coined by the author to describe the believer's rock-like firmness in Christ, echoing the biblical "rock" of faith.</note>, and that all these things will recoil upon those who cast them. Nor will any enemy easily deceive him with feigned love, as long as the Christian never turns his eyes away from the right and direct path of salvation; rather, he considers whatever does not savor of the soul and heaven to be hostile.

CHAPTER XII.

### *On offspring and fruitfulness.*

This threefold marriage is fruitful, and it fills heaven with a numerous progeny. The firstborn son is the *Highest Good* <note>original: *Summum bonum*</note>; then the daughters, *Justification* and *Blessedness*, are likewise born first. Their brothers and sisters are the entire chorus of human, Christian, and divine virtues. There shine forth among mortals the *Venerable* <note>original: *Augusti*</note>, the *Happy* <note>original: *Fœlices*</note>, the *Fortunate* <note>original: *Fausti*</note>, the *Peaceful* <note>original: *Friderici*</note>, the *Merciful* <note>original: *Clementes*</note>, the *Lively* <note>original: *Vitales*</note>, the *Victorious* <note>original: *Vincentij*</note>, the *Kind* <note>original: *Benigni*</note>, the *Free* <note>original: *Liberij*</note>, the *Gentle* <note>original: *Mansueti*</note>, the *Watchful* <note>original: *Vigilij*</note>, the *Resolute* <note>original: *Erhardi*</note>, the *Earnest* <note>original: *Ernesti*</note>, the *Strong* <note>original: *Valentes*</note>, and the *Great* <note>original: *Magni*</note>. There shine forth *Queens*, the *Bright*, the *Prudent*, the *True*, the *Harmonious*, the *Simple*, the *God-gifted*, the *God-given*, the *Gentle*, the *Ancient*, the *Joyful*, and the *Motherly* <note>The author uses a list of Latin names—such as Clara, Priscα, and Hilaria—that also function as adjectives for virtues</note>. All these, in the end, are the sons and daughters of God, subjects of the Kingdom of Christ, soldiers of the sacred militia, citizens of the heavenly city, and heirs to eternal goods. These are the grapes in the vineyard of the Lord; these are the apples of desire, the good fruits of a good tree, and of the divine Paradise

## CHAPTER XIII.
### *On the Retinue.* <note>The Latin *famulitium* refers to a body of servants or a household staff.</note>

If by chance anyone does not know how large the family of a Christian is, or how splendid his retinue, let him first realize that the *Angels*, those most powerful spirits, stand by him to watch over him and support his well-being with joyful applause. From there, the whole of *Nature* <note>original: *Natura*</note> serves him and is ready at his command or will; she supplies the tools for his intellect and allows him to emulate a kind of *God* on earth. Then, all *Philosophy* acts as a handmaid; all the arts and those things discovered through great labor provide service. Whatever dominates other mortals is a servant here. Furthermore, his own limbs serve him, and they do not so riot, indulge in luxury, or wander...

Finally, he even makes use of the hands and the readiness of his brothers, and from there, with the labors of each person joined together, the peaceful rule of the household is established.

## CHAPTER XIV.

### *On beasts and brutes.*

Astrologers <term>Astrologi: here referring to those who predict character or fate based on signs</term> are also accustomed to prophesy about beasts, which we shall not overlook—especially in this era, in which there is more of the beast among men than there is of humanity, and where one cannot easily recognize a human being even if a torch were lit at midday <note>This is a classical allusion to the philosopher Diogenes the Cynic, who carried a lamp in broad daylight searching for one true "man."</note>. Therefore, a Christian will have much to do with beasts. For one whom a face just now declared a man, he may soon find to be a cursing dog, a lewd goat <note>original: *hircum*</note>, an impure sow, an ignorant and lazy donkey <note>original: *asinum*</note>, or a spiteful ser-

[ser]pent he experiences. He will speak, but he will not be heard; he will teach, but they will learn nothing; he will pray, but he will obtain nothing. For the nourishment of such animals is merely opinion, habit, and their own will—but it is mostly poison and waste. They take such delight among these things that whoever speaks against them receives only grunting, barking, braying, and roaring in return. 

But Christ’s flock <note>original: *Christi grex*</note> consists of sheep, whose bodies are innocent and simple. They are most useful for their wool, milk, and offspring, and even after death for their hide, flesh, and sinews; they will not be refreshed except by the joyful and healthy pastures of the Divine Word <note>original: *verbi Divini*</note>. From these sheep, benefits come to our Renatus <note>“Renatus” means “Reborn,” likely the name of the central character or a stand-in for the ideal Christian reader.</note>, and no duty on earth or in heaven will be more profitable to him than the pastoral office <note>original: *pastorale munus*</note>—namely, that which is Patriarchal, Prophetic, Apostolic, and Christian.

### *On Teaching or Profession.*

AND who, finally, will be the one so greatly cared for by God and Nature? What office shall he fulfill? A truly noble one;

He is a *Physician* <note>original: *Medicus*</note> who, walking about everywhere, opens a vein <note>A reference to bloodletting, a common medical practice of the time intended to balance the "humors."</note> for this man, prescribes a diet for that one, and purges the stomach or brain of another.

Perhaps the Commonwealth <note>original: *Respub.*, referring to the "Republic" or the organized community of people</note> will lack a laborer or a craftsman, but in him, it will have an *Architect*.

## CHAPTER XVI.

### *On Dignities.*

These dignities <note>original: *Dignitates*; refers to high offices, honors, or ranks of state.</note> are also immense: *First, he is a King.* Nor does he only rule the Microcosm <note>The "small world"—a traditional philosophical concept where the individual human being reflects the structure of the entire universe.</note> in such a way that nothing is committed within it without his command, or in secret, or against the laws of his kingdom; he ensures that no punishment for the wicked nor reward for the good is lacking, and that no tumult or sedition arises. But he also takes from the Great World <note>The Macrocosm, or the physical universe.</note> whatever he needs by his own right; he commands his own fate and the stars; and from a sublime and heavenly citadel, he gazes upon and looks down at human affairs, being neither entangled nor moved by any of their disturbances.

Next, *he is a Judge*, and he judges most freely concerning all things that are and all things that happen. He is not swayed by a multitude of those who think differently, nor by greatness, nor by boasting; rather, with an even scale, he casts away the evil and stores the good within his Treasuries. 

Finally, *he is a Priest*, whose robe is the *Innocence of Christ*; his offerings are prayers; his sweet fragrances are examples of Charity <note>original: *Charitas*; the theological virtue of selfless love.</note>; his altar is his neighbor; his Temple is the Christian assembly; and his holy... <note>The text breaks here at the end of the page.</note>

## CHAPTER XVII.
## *On Fortune.*

What indeed can Fortune <note>original: *Fortuna*; the personification of luck, chance, or worldly fate, often depicted in art with a wheel to show how quickly she can raise someone up or cast them down.</note> do, or what might she threaten, to a man who is immovable and exempt from every authority except that of Christ? She has given nothing, she will take nothing away; she can play with her own riches, but never with those of the Christian. He guards his own possessions so that they remain his; those things that are foreign and outside of him, he leaves to the mockery of Fortune. Truly, in his Christ he is most fortunate; and while the rest of humanity seeks the Supreme Good <note>original: *Summum bonum*; the ultimate goal or highest end of human life in classical and Christian philosophy.</note> through infinite paths and windings, he himself possesses it securely. To be sure, the malice of Fortune will not be absent, but let those who wear purple <note>original: *purpurati*; refers to high-ranking officials, royalty, or the wealthy who wear expensive dyed robes—symbolizing those who have much to lose in the world.</note> fear her, not the Christian, who fears nothing. He neither seeks nor expects anything good from the World, for he has need of nothing other than Christ. Hence, while others load and adorn themselves with the gifts of Fortune: he himself strips himself even of things added voluntarily,

<summary>This page concludes the discussion of the believer's spiritual honors and introduces a meditation on Fortune, arguing that a true Christian is immune to the fluctuations of luck or worldly power because their treasure is in Christ.</summary>

so that he is never captured by the love of those things <note>Referring to the worldly gifts of Fortune mentioned on the previous page.</note>, nor submits himself to Fortune. He lives, therefore, not as one who loses this or that against his will, or who painfully lacks these things or those, but as one who at every moment is ready to leave behind the entire World and his "little hut" of a body <note>original: *tuguriolum suum corpus*; a humble metaphor for the physical body as a temporary shelter for the soul.</note> with a cheerful and ready spirit; and, having returned what was borrowed, he is about to enter into his own true inheritance.

## CHAPTER XVIII.
### *On Riches.*

Gold, coins, gems, stones, and metals are but filth; they do not constitute riches worthy of our Reborn Man <term>Renatus: A person who has been spiritually transformed or "born again" into a life of faith.</term>. Even whatever is produced by art and human ingenuity is not yet sufficient; indeed, it is far below the level of herbs, plants, and animals. For this reason, our Reborn Man has no desire for such things, and even less admiration or esteem for them—though the whole world, the learned and the unlearned alike, struggles for them. He possesses other, vast riches; indeed, his Wealth <note>original: *Census*; a term referring to a person's total estate or registered property.</note> is drawn from all created things and the multitude of Divine works. These are the Praise of God, [ex-...] <meta>catchword: ex-</meta>

To him, God, Nature, and all Creatures communicate their own riches.

# CHAPTER XX.

### *On Dreams.*

BUT even dreams <note>original: *insomnia*; while this often means sleeplessness today, in this context it refers to visions or dreams experienced during sleep.</note> the Astrologers claim for themselves; how much more truly does our Christian claim them, who will hold that which others call a "dream" to be the Sacred Word, a messenger of the Divine will. While the rest slumber and snore in their own vanity, dreaming of nothing but the images produced by the fumes of their opinions, habits, folly, and malice, this man of ours rests and sleeps entirely within the providence, protection, will, and commands of God. He delights in the revealed Divine counsel and in the purpose set forth for both the good and the wicked. No cares concerning his body, his possessions, or the schemes of his adversaries rouse him. His heart alone keeps watch for God; all else, intoxicated and lulled by a divine nectar <note>original: *divino liquore*; literally "divine liquid," a metaphor for the intoxicating peace or grace of God.</note>, passes through the night of this life so peacefully that they never suffer the horrors of darkness or experience the hauntings of phantoms <term>spectrorum: Apparitions or frightening spirits.</term>.

<page-num>229</page-num>
<sig>K 7</sig>

...until at last, with the dawn of eternity returning and Christ, the Sun of Justice <note>original: *Iustitiæ sole*; a reference to the "Sun of Righteousness" from Malachi 4:2, often associated with the Resurrection.</note>, being restored to the faithful, the awakened limbs may return to their duty with a new and increased eagerness. That is, they shall be made glorious for that perpetual rest which is yet active, that motion which is yet quiet, and for Christ, its Mover <term>motorique: A reference to the "Prime Mover," the source of all motion and life.</term>.

---

## THE FINAL CHAPTER
### *On Death.*

This world is a great charnel house <term>ossuarium: A place where the bones of the dead are collected; an ossuary.</term>, where we live as the dead among the dead—indeed, among those who despise the true life. And yet we still seek after death and complain about our own dying, though death is already within us and consumes us even through life and health themselves. For whatever time we believe we have added to our life, we have actually surrendered to death; even the hour of life that just passed is now dead. He who has completed a third of his life is already one-third dead; he who has completed half is half-dead. But the Christian is far different: he fears no death and acknowledges no death, but rather moves toward life out of death, in every [passing moment].

<vocab>Death, Christian, eternity, charnel house, Justice, Christ</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a meditation on the resurrection of the body and begins the final chapter on death, describing the world as a vast ossuary where every passing moment is a portion of life surrendered to death. It contrasts the worldly fear of death with the Christian's view of it as a progression toward true life.</summary>
<keywords>resurrection, Sun of Justice, Christ the Mover, death, ossuary, mortality, Christian hope, passage of time</keywords>

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Christian’s relationship with time and mortality. The previous page established that the Christian does not fear the passage of time, as every moment brings them closer to true life.</meta>

[each passing] moment closer. Therefore, what to others is terrifying—namely Death—and what to the more discerning is the end of a wretched life, is to him a summons <term>conclamatio: A formal calling out or a final signal, like a trumpet call, signaling the transition to a higher state.</term> to Life, to Liberty, to Happiness, and to Perfection. He hears that voice as gladly as he has composed his whole life to perceive and receive it; he has patiently endured the prison of his body, and considered all earthly things to be alien to him, and lent only for a fleeting moment. But while he lives, he has need of another death, by which *Adam* <note>Adam here represents the "Old Man" or the fallen, sinful nature of humanity that must be overcome.</note>—troublesome and hateful to God—is destroyed, every [personal] will is suffocated, and self-love <term>philautia: A Greek term for "self-love," used here to describe the spiritual ego or narcissism that separates a person from God.</term> is slain. Nor can he ever live unless he lives in another <note>Living "in another" refers to the mystical union where the believer lives "in Christ" rather than in their own ego.</note>, and dies to what is his own. This is the final, yet admirable and glorious *Paradox* of our Christian.

*Thus far concludes the Theosophical Judgment* <term>Iudicium Theosophicum: A spiritual assessment or "godly wisdom" concerning the state of the soul.</term> *concerning our Christian; that is, the vivid Image of a Man truly* Sojourning <note>original: *Hospitantis*; dwelling as a temporary guest or stranger in a foreign land.</note> *in these lands, and advancing on the heavenly journey: but one which we readily acknowledge will be ridiculous to the Citizens of the World* <term>Cosmopolitis: Literally "world-citizens," used here to describe those who are entirely devoted to secular life and dismissive of spiritual matters.</term>*. For to them, to perpetually remember oneself, Christ, and Death, is foolish...*

<summary>This page describes death not as a terror, but as a welcome summons to perfection for the Christian, who must undergo a "spiritual death" to self-love while still alive. It concludes the theological portrait of the spiritual pilgrim, noting that such a life appears ridiculous to those purely focused on the worldly realm.</summary>

<keywords>Death, spiritual rebirth, Adam, self-love, philautia, theosophy, spiritual pilgrimage, paradox of faith, worldliness</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Theosophical Judgment" regarding the true Christian, who is described as a "sojourner" or stranger on Earth. The previous page ended by noting that this spiritual path appears ridiculous to the "Citizens of the World."</meta>

to them it is, and it is diametrically opposed to the precepts of the World, which inculcates <unclear>nothing</unclear> but Fame, Wealth, Life, Memory, Labors, Arts, Subtleties, Splendor, and Titles. The World is nowhere a guide, a leader, or a precursor toward the straight and simple path of Piety—or at least toward that path which the Philosophers once trod. Instead, even the best counsels remain only in words, and the world’s play <note>original: *fabula*; referring to the "drama" or "fable" of worldly life as a stage performance.</note> and theatricality are repeatedly renewed.

Therefore, if you seek our *Cosmoxenus* <term>Cosmoxenus: From the Greek *kosmos* (world) and *xenos* (stranger), referring to the "World-Stranger" or the soul that does not belong to the earthly realm.</term>, you will find him nowhere except written or painted—that is to say, existing only on paper. He cries out to men in vain, for they are deaf; they cannot be restrained even a little from their headlong course of error until they have stumbled here and there more than a hundred thousand times.

O blessed are those whose eyes are opened early <note>original: *effringuntur*; literally "broken open," suggesting a forceful awakening from spiritual blindness.</note> and whose blocked ears are pierced, so that they might perceive the distinction between good and evil, the solid and the vain, the true and the false. With a more skillful and happy discernment, they may withdraw into a safe, secure, peaceful, free, and joyful place. There, they may pass the remaining moments <note>original: *puncta*; literally "points," emphasizing the fleeting, needle-prick brevity of human time.</note> of their life, rather than wandering until the very door of death—gray-haired, delirious, and blushing with va-

<margin>
World, Piety, Philosophers, World-Stranger, of error, vanity
</margin>

<summary>This page contrasts the hollow values of the world (fame and wealth) with the "World-Stranger" (Cosmoxenus), an ideal figure of piety who is ignored by the masses. It concludes with a blessing for those who awaken early enough to find spiritual peace before death.</summary>

<keywords>Cosmoxenus, worldly vanity, spiritual awakening, piety vs. philosophy, brevity of life, spiritual discernment</keywords>

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, completing the thought regarding those who realize the vanity of their lives only at the very end.</meta>

of vanity, error, misery, and all the burdens <note>original: *sarcinas*; literally "packs" or "luggage," here referring to the heavy spiritual weight of a worldly life.</note> of internal disturbances, and having lamented the course of a most unhappy life, they wish for themselves just a few remaining days of amendment and repentance.

But the matter is desperate, and no one rises up unless God lifts them up, nor does anyone cast off their burden unless God takes it away. Such is the unhappiness of the World that if today it were freed from the **Antichrist**, from **Muhammad** <note>original: *Mahomete*; in this 17th-century context, often used as a generic symbol for "false" religion or the perceived enemy of the Church.</note>, from **Mammon**, from **Fame**, from **Fortune**, and from **Aristotle** <note>The inclusion of the philosopher Aristotle suggests the author views rigid Scholastic logic as a spiritual "executioner" or a distraction from true piety.</note>—in a word, from **vanity**—tomorrow it would raise up new and even worse executioners. And so, despite the outcries of *Reason*, *Nature*, *Philosophy*, and *Theology*, the slaves of servitude will remain numerous; the children of Liberty will always be very few, most dejected, and ridiculed.

The only advantages remaining for attaining the right path are: the integrity of parents, the suitability of the place, the happiness of the time, the diligence of education, the conscientiousness of instructors, the prayers of the pious, the avoidance of temptation <note>original: *occasionis devitatio*; literally "the shunning of opportunity," referring to avoiding the "occasions of sin."</note>, the assiduity of labor, and the maturity of age. But above all things stands the *discipline of the Cross* <note>original: *Crucis exercitium*; the spiritual practice of suffering and self-denial.</note>, which so shatters the excess of our malice that even against our will, at last,

<summary>The author argues that humanity is so addicted to worldly "executioners" like fame and vanity that only divine intervention or the heavy discipline of the "Cross" can force a person toward spiritual repentance.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, divine grace, vanity, Aristotle, bondage of the soul, discipline of the cross, worldly misery</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion on the "discipline of the Cross," concluding the thought that true wisdom is found in accepting spiritual discipline rather than following worldly impulses.</meta>

…and let us grow wise under the yoke [of the Cross], and no longer be tormented by license and an abundance of every evil, but rather by the necessity of restraint. This struggle is both easier and leaves us as victors more quickly.

Whoever, therefore, feels those stirrings within themselves, such that they suffer a nausea for the **actions of the World** and realize they have already been raised to a more sublime and refined judgment regarding the affairs and pursuits of mortals: Let them first believe themselves blessed, and then destined for even greater **happiness**. Nor should they ever again submit themselves to the movements of the heavens, the positions <note>original: *aspectibus*; in astrology, "aspects" refers to the specific angles between planets, believed to influence human fate.</note> of the stars, the revolutions of the earth, the customs of men, their own appetites, or the habits of beasts. 

Instead, exulting in the privilege of **Christianity** <note>original: *Christianismus*; here referring to the status or "spiritual citizenship" of a Christian.</note>, and triumphing over all these things, let them not merely seek power, but grasp the dominion already offered by Christ, and make use of such a happy **Birth** <note>original: *Geniturâ*; in this context, the author is playing on the word for an astrological birth chart (geniture) to describe the "New Birth" or spiritual nature of a believer.</note> (as has been explained thus far). For no one is bound except by themselves and their own **vanity**, and no one serves more miserably or unworthily than those who serve themselves. Therefore, as soon as we have been **liberated** from ourselves, after we have...

->renun<- <note>Catchword for the following page: likely *renunciaverimus*, meaning "renounced."</note>

<summary>This page argues that spiritual maturity involves a "nausea" for worldly pursuits and a rejection of astrological or animalistic influences in favor of Christian liberty. It emphasizes that the greatest form of slavery is being bound to one's own vanity.</summary>
<keywords>Christianity, happiness, New Birth, worldliness, liberty, vanity, astrology, self-mastery</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the thought from the previous page regarding the liberation of the self from worldly vanity. The previous page ended with "after we have..." and this page completes the sentence with the act of renunciation.</meta>

*renounced those things which are not ours, nothing further remains that can prevent us from entering into **Perfect Happiness**.*

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Let others, therefore, seek elsewhere for whatever is truly good, or believed to be so; yet they will not find it, but will weary themselves by searching. Having become weary, they will lament; lamenting, they will despair; despairing, they will perish, nor will they extricate themselves from the chains of their evils. *We shall more readily seek rest, peace, tranquility, abundance, and knowledge within ourselves, and we shall obtain them most easily. For us, the **Trinity** will be the heavens, **Christ** the Sun, the **Church** the Moon, the **Teachers** the Planets, the **Choirs of the Elect** the Stars, **Antiquity** the First Motion <note>original: *Motus primus*; in the cosmology of the time, the "prime mover" was the outermost sphere that imparted motion to the rest of the universe.</note>, the signs of the times the second motions, the **Word of God** the Center, **Faith** the Fire, **sighs and prayers** the Air, **our own melting away** <note>original: *deliquium*; in a mystical context, this refers to the "failing" or "liquefaction" of the soul as it loses its ego in God.</note> the Water, **effort** the Earth, the **soul** the Day, and the **body** the Night. These things will be of far greater concern to us than that vast **Machine** <note>original: *Machina*; a common Early Modern term for the physical universe, or "world-machine," viewed as a grand construction of God.</note> fashioned for our decoration. By which **Astrology** <note>The author is proposing a "spiritual astrology," suggesting that studying the internal "stars" of virtue is more significant than studying the literal stars of the sky.</note> we shall shortly [obtain] that which is contrary and retrograde to the World...*

<meta>catchword: obtain- (from the Latin "impetrabitur" or "impetrabimus")</meta>

<summary>The author presents a mystical "spiritual microcosm," mapping theological concepts like the Trinity and Faith onto the physical structure of the universe and its elements. He argues that true peace is found by looking inward and studying the soul's "astrology" rather than the external, physical world.</summary>

<keywords>Perfect Happiness, spiritual microcosm, Christian mysticism, Word of God, spiritual astrology, soul and body, Trinity, internal peace</keywords>

...we are drawn <note>original: *[at]trabimus*; this verb completes the thought from the previous page, suggesting that we are pulled or guided by this spiritual "astrology."</note>, so that while the servants of this world <note>original: *eius servi*; referring to those who serve the "Machine" of the physical universe mentioned previously.</note> are ignorant in their knowing, are in want in their possessing, are weary in their resting, and are enslaved while being free; We, by our not-knowing, may know <note>A reference to the mystical tradition of "learned ignorance," where one gains divine knowledge by admitting the limits of human reason.</note>; by our acting, may possess; by our toiling, may find rest; and by our serving, we may be the
